You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Write a program which manipulates a sequence $A$ = {$a_0, a_1, ..., a_{n-1}$} with the following operations:

* $add(s, t, x)$ : add $x$ to $a_s, a_{s+1}, ..., a_t$.
* $find(s, t)$ : report the minimum value in $a_s, a_{s+1}, ..., a_t$.



Note that the initial values of $a_i ( i = 0, 1, ..., n-1 )$ are 0.

Constraints

* $1 ≤ n ≤ 100000$
* $1 ≤ q ≤ 100000$
* $0 ≤ s ≤ t < n$
* $-1000 ≤ x ≤ 1000$

Input


$n$ $q$
$query_1$
$query_2$
:
$query_q$


In the first line, $n$ (the number of elements in $A$) and $q$ (the number of queries) are given. Then, $i$th query $query_i$ is given in the following format:


0 $s$ $t$ $x$


or


1 $s$ $t$


The first digit represents the type of the query. '0' denotes $add(s, t, x)$ and '1' denotes $find(s, t)$.

Output

For each $find$ query, print the minimum value.

Example

Input

6 7
0 1 3 1
0 2 4 -2
1 0 5
1 0 1
0 3 5 3
1 3 4
1 0 5


Output

-2
0
1
-1

I'll solve this step by step using a segment tree with lazy propagation, which is efficient for range updates and range queries.

## Problem Understanding

We need to handle two operations on a sequence of integers:
1. **add(s, t, x)**: Add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. **find(s, t)**: Find the minimum value from index s to t (inclusive)

The initial values are all 0.

## Why Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation?

- We have up to 100,000 queries and 100,000 elements
- Each operation needs to work on ranges (not individual elements)
- We need to find minimum values efficiently
- A naive approach would be O(n) per query, which would be too slow
- Segment trees with lazy propagation can handle range updates and range queries in O(log n)

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Build a segment tree** that stores minimum values in ranges
2. **Use lazy propagation** to efficiently apply range additions
3. **Handle range updates** by updating the lazy values and propagating them down when needed
4. **Handle range queries** by finding the minimum in the specified range

## Implementation Details

- The segment tree will store the minimum value in each segment
- Lazy array will store pending additions that need to be applied to segments
- When updating a range, we modify the lazy values and update the tree structure
- When querying, we combine the results from child segments after applying any pending lazy values

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(300000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.tree = [0] * (4 * n)  # Stores minimum values
        self.lazy = [0] * (4 * n)  # Stores pending additions
    
    def push(self, node, left, right):
        """Push lazy values to children"""
        if self.lazy[node] != 0:
            # Apply lazy value to current node
            self.tree[node] += self.lazy[node]
            
            # If not a leaf node, push to children
            if left != right:
                self.lazy[2 * node] += self.lazy[node]
                self.lazy[2 * node + 1] += self.lazy[node]
                
            # Reset lazy value
            self.lazy[node] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, node, left, right, l, r, val):
        """Update range [l, r] by adding val"""
        # First push any pending lazy values
        self.push(node, left, right)
        
        # If current range doesn't overlap with update range
        if right < l or r < left:
            return
        
        # If current range is completely within update range
        if l <= left and right <= r:
            # Mark lazy value for this node
            self.lazy[node] += val
            # Push to ensure tree is up to date
            self.push(node, left, right)
            return
        
        # Otherwise, recursively update children
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        self.update_range(2 * node, left, mid, l, r, val)
        self.update_range(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, right, l, r, val)
        
        # Update current node with minimum of children
        self.tree[node] = min(self.tree[2 * node], self.tree[2 * node + 1])
    
    def query_range(self, node, left, right, l, r):
        """Query minimum in range [l, r]"""
        # First push any pending lazy values
        self.push(node, left, right)
        
        # If current range doesn't overlap with query range
        if right < l or r < left:
            return float('inf')  # Large value to represent no overlap
        
        # If current range is completely within query range
        if l <= left and right <= r:
            return self.tree[node]
        
        # Otherwise, recursively query children
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        left_min = self.query_range(2 * node, left, mid, l, r)
        right_min = self.query_range(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, right, l, r)
        
        return min(left_min, right_min)

# Read input and solve
n, q = map(int, input().split())

# Initialize segment tree
seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)

# Process queries
for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        seg_tree.update_range(1, 0, n - 1, s, t, x)
    else:  # find operation
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = seg_tree.query_range(1, 0, n - 1, s, t)
        print(result)
```

## Time Complexity Analysis

- **Update operation (add)**: O(log n) due to segment tree traversal
- **Query operation (find)**: O(log n) due to segment tree traversal
- **Total**: O(q log n) which is efficient for the given constraints

## Key Points in Implementation

1. **Lazy propagation**: Ensures we don't update every single element during range updates
2. **Segment tree structure**: Uses 0-based indexing and a complete binary tree representation
3. **Push operation**: Always applies pending updates before any further operations
4. **Tree updates**: When we need to update a range, we recursively descend and apply updates only when necessary
5. **Query logic**: When querying, we ensure the current node's values are up to date before returning results

The solution efficiently handles the given constraints and produces the expected output for the example.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to maintain a sequence A of n elements (initially all zeros) and support two operations:
1. `add(s, t, x)`: add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. `find(s, t)`: return the minimum value from index s to t (inclusive)

Constraints: up to 100,000 elements and 100,000 queries.

## Challenges

A naive approach of updating each element in the range for every add operation would be O(n) per add operation, leading to O(q*n) worst case, which might be too slow for 100,000 queries.

## Solution Approach

This is a classic range query problem that can be efficiently solved using a **Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation**.

### Why Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation?
- We need to perform range updates (add x to a range)
- We need to perform range queries (find minimum in a range)
- Lazy propagation allows us to defer updates until necessary, making both operations O(log n)

### Segment Tree Structure
- Each node represents a range of the array
- Stores the minimum value in that range
- Lazy array stores pending additions that need to be propagated

### Step-by-step Implementation

1. Build a segment tree with size 4*n (standard for segment trees)
2. For each add operation, update the lazy values using lazy propagation
3. For each find operation, query the minimum value with lazy propagation

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(300000)

def build_tree(idx, start, end):
    if start == end:
        tree[idx] = 0
    else:
        mid = (start + end) // 2
        build_tree(2 * idx + 1, start, mid)
        build_tree(2 * idx + 2, mid + 1, end)
        tree[idx] = min(tree[2 * idx + 1], tree[2 * idx + 2])

def push_lazy(idx, start, end):
    if lazy[idx] != 0:
        tree[idx] += lazy[idx]
        if start != end:
            lazy[2 * idx + 1] += lazy[idx]
            lazy[2 * idx + 2] += lazy[idx]
        lazy[idx] = 0

def update_range(idx, start, end, l, r, val):
    push_lazy(idx, start, end)
    if r < start or end < l:
        return
    
    if l <= start and end <= r:
        lazy[idx] += val
        push_lazy(idx, start, end)
        return
    
    mid = (start + end) // 2
    update_range(2 * idx + 1, start, mid, l, r, val)
    update_range(2 * idx + 2, mid + 1, end, l, r, val)
    tree[idx] = min(tree[2 * idx + 1], tree[2 * idx + 2])

def query_range(idx, start, end, l, r):
    push_lazy(idx, start, end)
    if r < start or end < l:
        return float('inf')
    
    if l <= start and end <= r:
        return tree[idx]
    
    mid = (start + end) // 2
    left_val = query_range(2 * idx + 1, start, mid, l, r)
    right_val = query_range(2 * idx + 2, mid + 1, end, l, r)
    return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input
n, q = map(int, input().split())

# Initialize segment tree and lazy arrays
tree = [0] * (4 * n)
lazy = [0] * (4 * n)

# Build initial tree (all zeros)
build_tree(0, 0, n - 1)

# Process queries
for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        update_range(0, 0, n - 1, s, t, x)
    else:  # find operation
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = query_range(0, 0, n - 1, s, t)
        print(result)
```

## Verification with Example

Initial: A = [0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 0]

1. `0 1 3 1`: add 1 to indices 1,2,3 → A = [0,1,1,1,0,0]
2. `0 2 4 -2`: add -2 to indices 2,3,4 → A = [0,1,-1,-1,-2,0]
3. `1 0 5`: min of [0,1,-1,-1,-2,0] = -2 ✓
4. `1 0 1`: min of [0,1] = 0 ✓
5. `0 3 5 3`: add 3 to indices 3,4,5 → A = [0,1,-1,2,1,3]
6. `1 3 4`: min of [2,1] = 1 ✓
7. `1 0 5`: min of [0,1,-1,2,1,3] = -1 ✓

All match expected output!

The solution uses segment tree with lazy propagation to achieve O(log n) per operation, making it efficient for the given constraints.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to maintain a sequence A of n elements (initially all 0) and support two operations:
1. `add(s, t, x)`: add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. `find(s, t)`: return the minimum value from index s to t (inclusive)

The challenge is that we have up to 100,000 queries, so we need an efficient solution.

## Approach Analysis

A naive approach would be to directly modify the array for each add operation and scan the range for each find operation. However, this would lead to O(n*q) worst-case time complexity, which might be too slow for the given constraints.

We need a more efficient data structure. A segment tree with lazy propagation would be ideal for range updates and range minimum queries (RMQ).

## Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation

The segment tree will store the minimum value in each segment and use lazy propagation to handle range updates efficiently.

Each node in the segment tree will store:
- The minimum value in its range
- A lazy value that represents pending additions to be applied to all elements in its range

When we perform a range update (add s to t with value x):
1. We update the lazy values in the relevant segments
2. We propagate the lazy values down when needed

When we query a range (find s to t):
1. We traverse the tree to find the minimum value in the range
2. We apply lazy values as we go down the tree

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Build a segment tree with size 4*n (standard for segment trees)
2. Implement functions for range update and range query
3. Use lazy propagation to defer updates
4. For each query, execute the appropriate operation

Let me implement this:

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1000000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.tree = [0] * (4 * n)
        self.lazy = [0] * (4 * n)
    
    def push(self, node, left, right):
        if self.lazy[node] != 0:
            # Apply lazy value to current node
            self.tree[node] += self.lazy[node]
            
            # If not a leaf, propagate to children
            if left != right:
                self.lazy[2 * node + 1] += self.lazy[node]
                self.lazy[2 * node + 2] += self.lazy[node]
            
            self.lazy[node] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, node, left, right, start, end, val):
        self.push(node, left, right)
        
        if right < start or end < left:
            return
        
        if start <= left and right <= end:
            self.lazy[node] += val
            self.push(node, left, right)
            return
        
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        self.update_range(2 * node + 1, left, mid, start, end, val)
        self.update_range(2 * node + 2, mid + 1, right, start, end, val)
        
        self.tree[node] = min(self.tree[2 * node + 1], self.tree[2 * node + 2])
    
    def query_range(self, node, left, right, start, end):
        self.push(node, left, right)
        
        if right < start or end < left:
            return float('inf')
        
        if start <= left and right <= end:
            return self.tree[node]
        
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(2 * node + 1, left, mid, start, end)
        right_val = self.query_range(2 * node + 2, mid + 1, right, start, end)
        
        return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input and process queries
n, q = map(int, input().split())

seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        seg_tree.update_range(0, 0, n - 1, s, t, x)
    else:  # find operation
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = seg_tree.query_range(0, 0, n - 1, s, t)
        print(result)
```

## Explanation of the Solution

1. **Segment Tree Structure**: We use a standard segment tree with 4*n size to store the minimum values.

2. **Lazy Propagation**: 
   - `push()` method applies pending updates to current node and propagates them to children if necessary
   - Updates are stored in `lazy[]` array

3. **Update Range (`update_range`)**:
   - First applies any pending lazy updates to current node
   - If the current range is completely within [start, end], we just update the lazy value
   - Otherwise, we recursively update left and right children

4. **Query Range (`query_range`)**:
   - Applies lazy updates before processing
   - Returns the minimum value in the queried range

5. **Time Complexity**: 
   - Each update and query operation is O(log n)
   - Overall complexity: O(q log n)

6. **Space Complexity**: O(n)

This solution efficiently handles the range updates and queries while maintaining the minimum value in any given range.

import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(1000000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.tree = [0] * (4 * n)
        self.lazy = [0] * (4 * n)
    
    def push(self, node, left, right):
        if self.lazy[node] != 0:
            # Apply lazy value to current node
            self.tree[node] += self.lazy[node]
            
            # If not a leaf, propagate to children
            if left != right:
                self.lazy[2 * node + 1] += self.lazy[node]
                self.lazy[2 * node + 2] += self.lazy[node]
            
            self.lazy[node] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, node, left, right, start, end, val):
        self.push(node, left, right)
        
        if right < start or end < left:
            return
        
        if start <= left and right <= end:
            self.lazy[node] += val
            self.push(node, left, right)
            return
        
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        self.update_range(2 * node + 1, left, mid, start, end, val)
        self.update_range(2 * node + 2, mid + 1, right, start, end, val)
        
        self.tree[node] = min(self.tree[2 * node + 1], self.tree[2 * node + 2])
    
    def query_range(self, node, left, right, start, end):
        self.push(node, left, right)
        
        if right < start or end < left:
            return float('inf')
        
        if start <= left and right <= end:
            return self.tree[node]
        
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(2 * node + 1, left, mid, start, end)
        right_val = self.query_range(2 * node + 2, mid + 1, right, start, end)
        
        return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input and process queries
n, q = map(int, input().split())

seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)

for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        seg_tree.update_range(0, 0, n - 1, s, t, x)
    else:  # find operation
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = seg_tree.query_range(0, 0, n - 1, s, t)
        print(result)

Write a program which manipulates a sequence $A$ = {$a_0, a_1, ..., a_{n-1}$} with the following operations:

* $add(s, t, x)$ : add $x$ to $a_s, a_{s+1}, ..., a_t$.
* $find(s, t)$ : report the minimum value in $a_s, a_{s+1}, ..., a_t$.

* $1 ≤ q ≤ 100000$

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to maintain a sequence A of n elements (initially all 0) and support two operations:
1. `add(s, t, x)`: add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. `find(s, t)`: find the minimum value in elements from index s to t (inclusive)

Constraints:
- n, q up to 100,000
- We need efficient operations since we have many queries

## Approach Analysis

Since we have range updates and range queries, we need a data structure that can handle both efficiently.

**Option 1: Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation**
- Can handle range updates and range minimum queries in O(log n) time
- Perfect for this problem with large constraints

**Option 2: Simple array with direct updates**
- O(n) for each add operation, O(n) for each find operation
- Would be too slow for n,q up to 100,000

**Option 3: Fenwick Tree**
- Better for point queries and range sums, not ideal for range minimum queries

Segment tree with lazy propagation is the best choice here.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Design the Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation**
   - Each node stores the minimum value in its range
   - Lazy propagation stores pending updates
   - For range updates, we add a value to a range
   - For range queries, we find the minimum in a range

2. **Implementation Details**
   - Tree will have 4*n nodes (0-indexed)
   - Lazy array will store pending additions
   - Need functions for: update range, query range, propagate lazy values

3. **Handle Updates and Queries**
   - For add operation: update range [s,t] with value x
   - For find operation: query minimum in range [s,t]

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(300000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.size = 1
        while self.size < n:
            self.size *= 2
        self.tree = [0] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
    
    def push(self, idx, l, r):
        if self.lazy[idx] != 0:
            self.tree[idx] += self.lazy[idx]
            if l != r:
                self.lazy[2 * idx] += self.lazy[idx]
                self.lazy[2 * idx + 1] += self.lazy[idx]
            self.lazy[idx] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, l, r, val, idx=1, seg_l=0, seg_r=None):
        if seg_r is None:
            seg_r = self.size - 1
        
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            self.lazy[idx] += val
            self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
            return
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        self.update_range(l, r, val, 2 * idx, seg_l, mid)
        self.update_range(l, r, val, 2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
        self.tree[idx] = min(self.tree[2 * idx], self.tree[2 * idx + 1])
    
    def query_range(self, l, r, idx=1, seg_l=0, seg_r=None):
        if seg_r is None:
            seg_r = self.size - 1
        
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return float('inf')
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            return self.tree[idx]
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(l, r, 2 * idx, seg_l, mid)
        right_val = self.query_range(l, r, 2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input
n, q = map(int, input().split())

# Create segment tree
seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)

# Process queries
for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        seg_tree.update_range(s, t, x)
    else:  # find operation
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = seg_tree.query_range(s, t)
        print(result)
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to maintain a sequence A of n elements (initially all 0) and support two operations:
1. `add(s, t, x)`: add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. `find(s, t)`: return the minimum value in elements from index s to t (inclusive)

Constraints: up to 100,000 elements and 100,000 queries

## Approach Analysis

### Naive Approach
For each add operation, we'd iterate from s to t and add x to each element. For find operations, we'd iterate from s to t to find the minimum. This would be O(n) per operation, leading to O(q*n) worst case - too slow for large inputs.

### Optimized Approach
Since we have range updates and range queries, a Segment Tree or Fenwick Tree with lazy propagation would be ideal. However, for minimum queries with range updates, we need to be careful.

Actually, let me think about simpler approaches first:

1. **Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation**: This can handle range updates and range queries efficiently in O(log n) time per operation.
2. **Fenwick Tree**: But Fenwick trees are more suited for sum queries, not minimum queries.
3. **Sweep-line/Coordinate Compression**: Not applicable here.

Given the constraints, a segment tree with lazy propagation is the most suitable solution, but it's complex to implement.

Wait, there's a simpler observation: since we're only dealing with additive operations and finding minima, we could use a simple array and update only when necessary. However, worst case might still be too slow.

Actually, let me reconsider. The problem has range updates and range minimum queries. This is a classic application of a Segment Tree with lazy propagation.

But let's think about the constraints: 100,000 operations on 100,000 elements. A naive approach might work in Python if the average case is favorable, but worst case could be too slow.

Since this is a programming competition problem, I'll implement a Segment Tree with lazy propagation for range updates and range minimum queries.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build a Segment Tree that supports range updates (adding a value to a range) and range minimum queries
2. Each node stores the minimum value in its range
3. Use lazy propagation to defer updates until necessary
4. For each add operation, update the lazy values
5. For each find operation, query the minimum in the range

Let me implement the segment tree with lazy propagation:

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(200000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.tree = [0] * (4 * n)
        self.lazy = [0] * (4 * n)
    
    def push(self, node, start, end):
        if self.lazy[node] != 0:
            self.tree[node] += self.lazy[node]
            if start != end:
                self.lazy[node * 2] += self.lazy[node]
                self.lazy[node * 2 + 1] += self.lazy[node]
            self.lazy[node] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, node, start, end, l, r, val):
        self.push(node, start, end)
        if r < start or end < l:
            return
        if l <= start and end <= r:
            self.lazy[node] += val
            self.push(node, start, end)
            return
        
        mid = (start + end) // 2
        self.update_range(node * 2, start, mid, l, r, val)
        self.update_range(node * 2 + 1, mid + 1, end, l, r, val)
        self.tree[node] = min(self.tree[node * 2], self.tree[node * 2 + 1])
    
    def query_range(self, node, start, end, l, r):
        self.push(node, start, end)
        if r < start or end < l:
            return float('inf')
        if l <= start and end <= r:
            return self.tree[node]
        
        mid = (start + end) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(node * 2, start, mid, l, r)
        right_val = self.query_range(node * 2 + 1, mid + 1, end, l, r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)

def solve():
    n, q = map(int, input().split())
    
    seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)
    
    for _ in range(q):
        query = list(map(int, input().split()))
        if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
            s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
            seg_tree.update_range(1, 0, n-1, s, t, x)
        else:  # find operation
            s, t = query[1], query[2]
            result = seg_tree.query_range(1, 0, n-1, s, t)
            print(result)

solve()
```

0 $s$ $t$ $x$

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to maintain a sequence A of n elements (initially all 0) and support two operations:
1. `add(s, t, x)`: add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. `find(s, t)`: return the minimum value in the range from index s to t (inclusive)

The constraints are large (up to 100,000 elements and queries), so we need an efficient data structure.

## Analysis

A naive approach would be to update each element in the range for add operations and scan the range for find operations. However, this would be O(n) per operation, leading to O(n*q) worst case which might be too slow for the given constraints.

We need a more efficient approach. A segment tree with lazy propagation would be ideal here, as it can handle range updates and range queries efficiently.

## Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation

The segment tree will support:
- Range updates (adding x to a range)
- Range minimum queries

Key components:
- Each node stores the minimum value in its range
- Lazy propagation stores pending updates that need to be applied to children

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build a segment tree with initial values all 0
2. For each add operation, update the segment tree with lazy propagation
3. For each find operation, query the minimum value in the specified range

## Implementation

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(200000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.size = 1
        while self.size < n:
            self.size *= 2
        self.tree = [0] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
    
    def push(self, idx, l, r):
        if self.lazy[idx] != 0:
            self.tree[idx] += self.lazy[idx]
            if l != r:
                self.lazy[idx * 2] += self.lazy[idx]
                self.lazy[idx * 2 + 1] += self.lazy[idx]
            self.lazy[idx] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, l, r, val, idx=1, seg_l=0, seg_r=None):
        if seg_r is None:
            seg_r = self.size - 1
        
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            self.lazy[idx] += val
            self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
            return
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        self.update_range(l, r, val, idx * 2, seg_l, mid)
        self.update_range(l, r, val, idx * 2 + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
        self.tree[idx] = min(self.tree[idx * 2], self.tree[idx * 2 + 1])
    
    def query_range(self, l, r, idx=1, seg_l=0, seg_r=None):
        if seg_r is None:
            seg_r = self.size - 1
        
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return float('inf')
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            return self.tree[idx]
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(l, r, idx * 2, seg_l, mid)
        right_val = self.query_range(l, r, idx * 2 + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input
n, q = map(int, input().split())

# Create segment tree
seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)

# Process queries
for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        seg_tree.update_range(s, t, x)
    else:  # find operation
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = seg_tree.query_range(s, t)
        print(result)
```

## Explanation of the Code

1. **SegmentTree Class**: 
   - `size` is the smallest power of 2 >= n to accommodate the array
   - `tree` stores the minimum value for each segment
   - `lazy` stores pending updates that should be propagated

2. **push method**: Propagates lazy updates down the tree. If a node has a pending update, it applies it to its value and to its children's lazy values.

3. **update_range method**: Updates a range with a value. It uses lazy propagation to avoid updating every single element.

4. **query_range method**: Queries the minimum value in a range. It uses lazy propagation to ensure we have the current values.

5. **Time Complexity**: 
   - Each operation (add or find) is O(log n)
   - Overall: O(q log n), which is efficient for the given constraints

6. **Space Complexity**: O(n)

The solution handles the example correctly:
- Initially all zeros
- After `add(1,3,1)`: positions 1,2,3 become 1
- After `add(2,4,-2)`: positions 2,3,4 become -1, 1, -1
- `find(0,5)`: minimum is -2 (at position 4)
- `find(0,1)`: minimum is 0 (positions 0,1 are 0,1)
- After `add(3,5,3)`: positions 3,4,5 become 2,2,3
- `find(3,4)`: minimum is 1 (position 3 is 2, position 4 is 2? Wait, let me recalculate...)

Let me trace through the example more carefully:

Initial: [0,0,0,0,0,0]

1. `add(1,3,1)`: [0,1,1,1,0,0]
2. `add(2,4,-2)`: [0,1,-1,-1,-2,0]
3. `find(0,5)`: min is -2 ✓
4. `find(0,1)`: min is 0 ✓
5. `add(3,5,3)`: [0,1,-1,3,1,3]
6. `find(3,4)`: min is 1 ✓
7. `find(0,5)`: min is -1 ✓

Wait, position 4 after step 5 should be: -2 + 3 = 1, yes. And position 3: 1 + 3 = 4? No.

After step 2: positions are [0,1,-1,-1,-2,0]
After step 5: add(3,5,3) means positions 3,4,5 get +3
So: position 3: -1 + 3 = 2, position 4: -2 + 3 = 1, position 5: 0 + 3 = 3
So we get [0,1,-1,2,1,3]
Minimum from 3,4 is min(2,1) = 1 ✓
Minimum from 0,5 is min(0,1,-1,2,1,3) = -1 ✓

The code is correct.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to maintain a sequence A of n elements (initially all 0) and support two operations:
1. `add(s, t, x)`: add x to all elements from index s to t (inclusive)
2. `find(s, t)`: find the minimum value in the range from index s to t (inclusive)

Constraints: n, q ≤ 100,000, so we need an efficient solution.

## Key Challenge

If we simply update each element one by one for every add operation, the worst case could be O(n*q) which might be too slow for 100,000 queries.

## Solution Approach

This is a classic problem that can be solved efficiently using a **Fenwick Tree (Binary Indexed Tree)** or **Segment Tree** with lazy propagation. However, since we only need to find minimum values and updates are range additions, we can use a simpler approach with **Difference Array + Offline Queries** or **Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation**.

Given the constraints, a Segment Tree with lazy propagation would be most suitable as it can handle both range updates and range queries efficiently in O(log n) time.

But for simplicity and given that we only need minimum values, I'll use a **Segment Tree with Lazy Propagation** specifically designed for range updates (addition) and range queries (minimization).

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Build a segment tree that supports range updates (adding a value) and range queries (finding minimum)
2. For each add operation, update the range [s,t] by adding x
3. For each find operation, query the minimum value in range [s,t]
4. Return the minimum value for each find operation

## Implementation Details

- The segment tree will store minimum values
- Lazy propagation will store pending updates (the value to be added to each range)
- When updating a range, we add the value to the lazy array and propagate the changes
- When querying, we retrieve the minimum value considering the lazy updates

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(300000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.size = 1
        while self.size < n:
            self.size *= 2
        # tree stores minimum values, lazy stores pending additions
        self.tree = [0] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
    
    def push(self, node, left, right):
        if self.lazy[node] != 0:
            # Add the lazy value to current node
            self.tree[node] += self.lazy[node]
            # If not a leaf, propagate to children
            if left != right:
                self.lazy[2 * node] += self.lazy[node]
                self.lazy[2 * node + 1] += self.lazy[node]
            # Reset lazy value
            self.lazy[node] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, node, left, right, l, r, val):
        self.push(node, left, right)
        if r < left or right < l:
            return
        if l <= left and right <= r:
            self.lazy[node] += val
            self.push(node, left, right)
            return
        
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        self.update_range(2 * node, left, mid, l, r, val)
        self.update_range(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, right, l, r, val)
        self.tree[node] = min(self.tree[2 * node], self.tree[2 * node + 1])
    
    def query_range(self, node, left, right, l, r):
        self.push(node, left, right)
        if r < left or right < l:
            return float('inf')
        if l <= left and right <= r:
            return self.tree[node]
        
        mid = (left + right) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(2 * node, left, mid, l, r)
        right_val = self.query_range(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, right, l, r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)

def solve():
    n, q = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Initialize segment tree
    seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)
    
    for _ in range(q):
        query = list(map(int, input().split()))
        
        if query[0] == 0:  # add operation
            s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
            seg_tree.update_range(1, 0, seg_tree.size - 1, s, t, x)
        else:  # find operation
            s, t = query[1], query[2]
            result = seg_tree.query_range(1, 0, seg_tree.size - 1, s, t)
            print(result)

solve()
```

```python
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
```

## Solution Approach

1. Build a segment tree that supports:

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(200000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.size = 1
        while self.size < n:
            self.size *= 2
        self.tree = [0] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
    
    def push(self, idx, l, r):
        if self.lazy[idx] != 0:
            self.tree[idx] += self.lazy[idx]
            if l != r:
                self.lazy[2 * idx] += self.lazy[idx]
                self.lazy[2 * idx + 1] += self.lazy[idx]
            self.lazy[idx] = 0
    
    def update_range(self, l, r, val, idx=1, seg_l=0, seg_r=None):
        if seg_r is None:
            seg_r = self.size - 1
        
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            self.lazy[idx] += val
            self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
            return
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        self.update_range(l, r, val, 2 * idx, seg_l, mid)
        self.update_range(l, r, val, 2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
        self.tree[idx] = min(self.tree[2 * idx], self.tree[2 * idx + 1])
    
    def query_range(self, l, r, idx=1, seg_l=0, seg_r=None):
        if seg_r is None:
            seg_r = self.size - 1
        
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return float('inf')
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            return self.tree[idx]
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        left_val = self.query_range(l, r, 2 * idx, seg_l, mid)
        right_val = self.query_range(l, r, 2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input
n, q = map(int, input().split())

    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
```

```python
class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):

            if l != r:

            self.lazy[idx] = 0

        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:

            return

        self.update_range(l, r, val, 2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r)
```

Given that worst-case might be too slow, let me implement the segment tree properly:

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(200000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.size = 1
        while self.size < n:
            self.size *= 2
        self.tree = [0] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
    
    def push(self, idx, l, r):
        if self.lazy[idx] != 0:
            self.tree[idx] += self.lazy[idx]
            if l != r:
                self.lazy[2 * idx] += self.lazy[idx]
                self.lazy[2 * idx + 1] += self.lazy[idx]
            self.lazy[idx] = 0
    
    def update(self, l, r, val):
        self._update(1, 0, self.size - 1, l, r, val)
    
    def _update(self, idx, seg_l, seg_r, l, r, val):
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            self.lazy[idx] += val
            self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
            return
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        self._update(2 * idx, seg_l, mid, l, r, val)
        self._update(2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r, l, r, val)
        self.tree[idx] = min(self.tree[2 * idx], self.tree[2 * idx + 1])
    
    def query(self, l, r):
        return self._query(1, 0, self.size - 1, l, r)
    
    def _query(self, idx, seg_l, seg_r, l, r):
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return float('inf')
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            return self.tree[idx]
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        left_val = self._query(2 * idx, seg_l, mid, l, r)
        right_val = self._query(2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r, l, r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)

# Read input
n, q = map(int, input().split())

# Create segment tree
seg_tree = SegmentTree(n)

# Process queries
for _ in range(q):
    query = list(map(int, input().split()))
    if query[0] == 0:
        s, t, x = query[1], query[2], query[3]
        seg_tree.update(s, t, x)
    else:
        s, t = query[1], query[2]
        result = seg_tree.query(s, t)
        print(result)
```

I think this should work. Let me verify with the example:
- Initially: [0,0,0,0,0,0]
- 0 1 3 1: add 1 to indices 1,2,3 → [0,1,1,1,0,0]
- 0 2 4 -2: add -2 to indices 2,3,4 → [0,1,-1,-1,-2,0]
- 1 0 5: min of entire array = -2 ✓
- 1 0 1: min of indices 0,1 = min(0,1) = 0 ✓
- 0 3 5 3: add 3 to indices 3,4,5 → [0,1,-1,4,1,3]
- 1 3 4: min of indices 3,4 = min(4,1) = 1 ✓
- 1 0 5: min of entire array = min(0,1,-1,4,1,3) = -1 ✓

```python
import sys
sys.setrecursionlimit(200000)

class SegmentTree:
    def __init__(self, n):
        self.n = n
        self.size = 1
        while self.size < n:
            self.size *= 2
        self.tree = [0] * (2 * self.size)
        self.lazy = [0] * (2 * self.size)
    
    def push(self, idx, l, r):
        if self.lazy[idx] != 0:
            self.tree[idx] += self.lazy[idx]
            if l != r:
                self.lazy[2 * idx] += self.lazy[idx]
                self.lazy[2 * idx + 1] += self.lazy[idx]
            self.lazy[idx] = 0
    
    def update(self, l, r, val):
        self._update(1, 0, self.size - 1, l, r, val)
    
    def _update(self, idx, seg_l, seg_r, l, r, val):
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            self.lazy[idx] += val
            self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
            return
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        self._update(2 * idx, seg_l, mid, l, r, val)
        self._update(2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r, l, r, val)
        self.tree[idx] = min(self.tree[2 * idx], self.tree[2 * idx + 1])
    
    def query(self, l, r):
        return self._query(1, 0, self.size - 1, l, r)
    
    def _query(self, idx, seg_l, seg_r, l, r):
        self.push(idx, seg_l, seg_r)
        
        if r < seg_l or seg_r < l:
            return float('inf')
        
        if l <= seg_l and seg_r <= r:
            return self.tree[idx]
        
        mid = (seg_l + seg_r) // 2
        left_val = self._query(2 * idx, seg_l, mid, l, r)
        right_val = self._query(2 * idx + 1, mid + 1, seg_r, l, r)
        return min(left_val, right_val)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi wants to grill N pieces of meat on a grilling net, which can be seen as a two-dimensional plane. The coordinates of the i-th piece of meat are \left(x_i, y_i\right), and its hardness is c_i.
Takahashi can use one heat source to grill the meat. If he puts the heat source at coordinates \left(X, Y\right), where X and Y are real numbers, the i-th piece of meat will be ready to eat in c_i \times \sqrt{\left(X - x_i\right)^2 + \left(Y-y_i\right)^2} seconds.
Takahashi wants to eat K pieces of meat. Find the time required to have K or more pieces of meat ready if he put the heat source to minimize this time.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 60
 - 1 \leq K \leq N
 - -1000 \leq x_i , y_i \leq 1000
 - \left(x_i, y_i\right) \neq \left(x_j, y_j\right) \left(i \neq j \right)
 - 1 \leq c_i \leq 100

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
x_1 y_1 c_1
\vdots
x_N y_N c_N

-----Output-----
Print the answer.
It will be considered correct if its absolute or relative error from our answer is at most 10^{-6}.

-----Sample Input-----
4 3
-1 0 3
0 0 3
1 0 2
1 1 40

-----Sample Output-----
2.4

If we put the heat source at \left(-0.2, 0\right), the 1-st, 2-nd, and 3-rd pieces of meat will be ready to eat within 2.4 seconds. This is the optimal place to put the heat source.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N pieces of meat at coordinates (x_i, y_i) with hardness c_i
2. We place a heat source at (X, Y) and the i-th piece takes c_i × √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²) seconds to cook
3. We want to minimize the time when K pieces of meat are ready
4. We need to find the optimal position (X, Y) that minimizes the maximum cooking time across K pieces

## Key Insights

1. This is a geometric optimization problem where we need to find the position that minimizes the maximum cooking time for K pieces
2. For a fixed time T, we can check if it's possible to have K pieces ready within time T
3. For each piece i, if we have time T, then the heat source must be within distance T/c_i from (x_i, y_i)
4. So for time T, piece i is ready if (X - x_i)² + (Y - y_i)² ≤ (T/c_i)²
5. This defines a circle of radius T/c_i centered at (x_i, y_i)
6. We need at least K such circles to overlap (have a common intersection point)

## Solution Approach

1. Use binary search on the answer (time T)
2. For each candidate time T, check if there exists a point (X, Y) such that K pieces are ready within time T
3. To check if K pieces can be ready within time T:
   - For each piece i, create a circle of radius T/c_i centered at (x_i, y_i)
   - Check if there's a common intersection point of at least K of these circles
4. To check circle intersection: two circles intersect if the distance between centers ≤ sum of radii and ≥ |r1 - r2|
5. For more than 2 circles, we need to check if there's a point inside all K circles simultaneously

However, since N ≤ 60, we can use a simpler approach:

For a fixed time T, we need to check if there's a point (X, Y) such that at least K pieces satisfy the distance constraint. This is equivalent to checking if the intersection of K circles is non-empty.

A more practical approach:
- Since the optimal solution likely occurs at a point that is equidistant to some subset of points (or at a vertex of the intersection), we can try all combinations of points and check if their intersection gives us the solution.

But given the constraints and the fact that we're dealing with continuous optimization, a simpler and more reliable approach is:

**Binary search on time T, and for each T, check if there's a point (X, Y) such that at least K pieces satisfy (X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)² ≤ (T/c_i)²**

To check if such a point exists, we can use the fact that if we have a set of circles, they have a common intersection if and only if every pair of circles intersects (for the necessary condition) and the entire system is feasible.

Actually, for this problem, we can use the following insight: the optimal point where K pieces are ready simultaneously will likely be at one of the points where the gradients of the distance constraints are balanced. However, since N is small, we can use binary search and verify feasibility.

A more practical approach:
- Binary search on the time T
- For each T, check if there exists a point (X, Y) such that at least K pieces satisfy the distance constraint
- To check this efficiently, we can try all combinations of K pieces and see if their minimal intersection is non-empty, or use a geometric approach

However, the simplest and most robust approach for this problem size is to use binary search on T, and for each T, check feasibility by seeing if there's a point inside K circles.

We can use the following geometric fact: a point is inside all K circles if and only if it's in the intersection of all K circles. We can check this by finding if there's any point in the intersection of any K circles by checking pairwise intersections.

But for simplicity and correctness, I'll use a binary search approach with a feasibility check that tries to find if a point exists in the intersection of K circles.

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_squared(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt(distance_squared(p1, p2))

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    return distance(point, center) <= radius

def circles_intersect(p1, r1, p2, r2):
    d = distance(p1, p2)
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False, None
    if d == 0 and r1 == r2:
        return True, None
    if d == 0 and r1 != r2:
        return False, None
    
    # Find intersection points of two circles
    # Using standard formula
    a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - a*a)
    
    # Center of the base line
    x = p1[0] + a * (p2[0] - p1[0]) / d
    y = p1[1] + a * (p2[1] - p1[1]) / d
    
    # Two intersection points
    x1 = x + h * (p2[1] - p1[1]) / d
    y1 = y - h * (p2[0] - p1[0]) / d
    
    x2 = x - h * (p2[1] - p1[1]) / d
    y2 = y + h * (p2[0] - p1[0]) / d
    
    return True, ((x1, y1), (x2, y2))

def circles_intersection_exists(circles):
    # First check if any pair intersects
    n = len(circles)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            center1, r1 = circles[i]
            center2, r2 = circles[j]
            if not circles_intersect(center1, r1, center2, r2)[0]:
                return False
    
    # If all pairs intersect, check if there's a common point
    # For simplicity, we'll check if the intersection of first two circles intersects with others
    if n <= 2:
        return True
    
    # Take first two circles intersection points
    first_pair = circles[0]
    second_pair = circles[1]
    centers = [first_pair[0], second_pair[0]]
    radii = [first_pair[1], second_pair[1]]
    
    valid_points = []
    ok, points = circles_intersect(centers[0], radii[0], centers[1], radii[1])
    if ok:
        valid_points.extend(points)
    
    # Check if any of these points lies in all other circles
    for check_point in valid_points:
        all_in = True
        for i in range(2, n):
            center, radius = circles[i]
            if not point_in_circle(check_point, center, radius):
                all_in = False
                break
        if all_in:
            return True
    
    return False

def feasible(T, meats, K):
    # For each meat, compute the circle radius T/c_i
    circles = []
    for i, (x, y, c) in enumerate(meats):
        radius = T / c
        circles.append(((x, y), radius))
    
    # Try all combinations of K pieces
    for combo in combinations(range(len(meats)), K):
        circle_list = []
        for idx in combo:
            x, y, c = meats[idx]
            radius = T / c
            circle_list.append(((x, y), radius))
        
        # Check if these K circles have a common intersection
        if circles_intersection_exists(circle_list):
            return True
    
    return False

# Read input
N, K = map(int, input().split())
meats = []
for _ in range(N):
    x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
    meats.append((x, y, c))

# Binary search on time
left, right = 0.0, 100000.0
eps = 1e-9

while right - left > eps:
    mid = (left + right) / 2.0
    if feasible(mid, meats, K):
        right = mid
    else:
        left = mid

print(f"{left:.15f}")
```

Wait, I need to reconsider this. The intersection check for multiple circles is complex and might be overkill. Also, the circle intersection function I implemented has issues.

Let me simplify and reconsider. The key insight is that if we have K pieces and a time T, then all K pieces must be within their respective circles. A point exists that lies in all K circles if and only if the intersection is non-empty.

For two circles, we have the intersection formula. For multiple circles, we can check if the intersection of any K circles is non-empty by checking if their pairwise intersections are consistent.

But a simpler approach for this problem size is to use a geometric median-like approach or to recognize that the optimal point often lies at a special position.

Actually, let me implement a cleaner version with binary search and a more robust feasibility function:

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circles_intersect(p1, r1, p2, r2):
    d = distance(p1, p2)
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False
    return True

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    return distance(point, center) <= radius

def feasible(T, meats, K):
    # For each meat, compute the circle radius T/c_i
    n = len(meats)
    
    # Try all combinations of K pieces
    for combo in combinations(range(n), K):
        # Get the K circles with radius T/c_i for each piece
        circles = []
        for idx in combo:
            x, y, c = meats[idx]
            radius = T / c
            circles.append(((x, y), radius))
        
        # Check if these K circles have a common intersection
        # We'll check if any intersection point of the first two circles is in all others
        if len(circles) == 1:
            return True
            
        # Find intersection of first two circles
        center1, r1 = circles[0]
        center2, r2 = circles[1]
        
        if not circles_intersect(center1, r1, center2, r2):
            continue
            
        # Calculate intersection points
        d = distance(center1, center2)
        a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
        h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - a*a)
        
        # Direction vector from center1 to center2
        dx = center2[0] - center1[0]
        dy = center2[1] - center1[1]
        unit_dx = dx / d
        unit_dy = dy / d
        
        # Intersection points
        x1 = center1[0] + a * unit_dx
        y1 = center1[1] + a * unit_dy
        x2 = center1[0] - a * unit_dx
        y2 = center1[1] - a * unit_dy
        
        # Check if any intersection point lies in all other circles
        for (x,y), radius in circles[2:]:
            if not point_in_circle((x1, y1), (x, y), radius) and not point_in_circle((x2, y2), (x, y), radius):
                break
        else:
            return True
            
    return False

# Read input
N, K = map(int, input().split())
meats = []
for _ in range(N):
    x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
    meats.append((x, y, c))

# Binary search on time
left, right = 0.0, 100000.0
eps = 1e-8

while right - left > eps:
    mid = (left + right) / 2.0
    if feasible(mid, meats, K):
        right = mid
    else:
        left = mid

print(f"{left:.15f}")
```

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False
    return True

    return distance(point, center) <= radius

        if len(circles) == 1:

        # Find intersection of first two circles

        center2, r2 = circles[1]
        
        if not circles_intersect(center1, r1, center2, r2):
            continue
            
        # Calculate intersection points
        d = distance(center1, center2)
        a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)

        unit_dx = dx / d
        unit_dy = dy / d

        x1 = center1[0] + a * unit_dx
        y1 = center1[1] + a * unit_dy
        x2 = center1[0] - a * unit_dx
        y2 = center1[1] - a * unit_dy
        
        # Check if any intersection point lies in all other circles
        for (x,y), radius in circles[2:]:

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi wants to grill N pieces of meat on a grilling net, which can be seen as a two-dimensional plane. The coordinates of the i-th piece of meat are \left(x_i, y_i\right), and its hardness is c_i.
Takahashi can use one heat source to grill the meat. If he puts the heat source at coordinates \left(X, Y\right), where X and Y are real numbers, the i-th piece of meat will be ready to eat in c_i \times \sqrt{\left(X - x_i\right)^2 + \left(Y-y_i\right)^2} seconds.
Takahashi wants to eat K pieces of meat. Find the time required to have K or more pieces of meat ready if he put the heat source to minimize this time.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 60
 - 1 \leq K \leq N
 - -1000 \leq x_i , y_i \leq 1000
 - \left(x_i, y_i\right) \neq \left(x_j, y_j\right) \left(i \neq j \right)
 - 1 \leq c_i \leq 100

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
x_1 y_1 c_1
\vdots
x_N y_N c_N

-----Output-----
Print the answer.
It will be considered correct if its absolute or relative error from our answer is at most 10^{-6}.

-----Sample Input-----
4 3
-1 0 3
0 0 3
1 0 2
1 1 40

-----Sample Output-----
2.4

If we put the heat source at \left(-0.2, 0\right), the 1-st, 2-nd, and 3-rd pieces of meat will be ready to eat within 2.4 seconds. This is the optimal place to put the heat source.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

```python
import math
import sys

def distance_sq(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt(distance_sq(p1, p2))

def solve():
    # Read input
    line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    if not line:
        return
    N, K = map(int, line.split())
    
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If K == 1, we just need to find the minimum time to cook one piece
    # The optimal position is at the meat itself, so time = c_i * 0 = 0
    # But actually, we can place heat source anywhere, so minimum time is 0?
    # Wait, no: if we place heat source at meat i, time for piece i is 0
    # So for K=1, answer is 0
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    # Upper bound: worst case, place heat source far away, but we can bound it
    # Maximum distance: 2000 (coordinates from -1000 to 1000), max c_i = 100
    # So worst case distance = 100 * sqrt(2000^2 + 2000^2) = 100 * 2000 * sqrt(2) ≈ 282840
    # But we can use a larger bound
    left, right = 0.0, 300000.0
    
    # We'll do binary search until precision is good enough
    eps = 1e-9
    while right - left > eps:
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check if time mid is feasible: is there a point that is within mid/c_i of at least K pieces?
        feasible = False
        
        # Check all pairwise intersection points
        # Also check the centers for completeness
        centers = [(x, y) for x, y, c in meats]
        
        # Check centers
        for i in range(N):
            center_x, center_y = centers[i]
            count = 0
            for j in range(N):
                xj, yj, cj = meats[j]
                dist_sq = distance_sq((center_x, center_y), (xj, yj))
                dist = math.sqrt(dist_sq)
                required_radius = mid / cj
                if dist <= required_radius:
                    count += 1
            if count >= K:
                feasible = True
                break
        
        if feasible:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.10f}")

solve()
```

Wait, this approach has a problem: for a given T, we're checking if there's a point within distance T/c_i of at least K pieces. But our feasibility check for centers might miss the optimal solution. Also, we're only checking the centers, and pairwise intersection points.

I need to compute the pairwise intersection points properly.

Let me fix this:

For two circles:
- Circle 1: center (x1,y1), radius r1 = T/c1
- Circle 2: center (x2,y2), radius r2 = T/c2

The intersection points can be computed geometrically.

The distance between centers d = sqrt((x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2)

If d > r1 + r2 or d < |r1 - r2|, no intersection.

Otherwise, we can compute the intersection points.

```python
import math
import sys

def distance_sq(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt(distance_sq(p1, p2))

def solve():
    # Read input
    line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    if not line:
        return
    N, K = map(int, line.split())
    
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, 300000.0
    eps = 1e-9
    
    while right - left > eps:
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check feasibility: is there a point that is within mid/c_i of at least K pieces?
        feasible = False
        
        # We'll check: 
        # 1. The centers of the pieces
        # 2. The intersection points of pairs of circles
        
        # Check centers
        for i in range(N):
            center_x, center_y = meats[i][0], meats[i][1]
            count = 0
            for j in range(N):
                xj, yj, cj = meats[j]
                dist = distance((center_x, center_y), (xj, yj))
                radius_required = mid / cj
                if dist <= radius_required:
                    count += 1
            if count >= K:
                feasible = True
                break
        
        if not feasible:
            # Check pairwise intersection points
            for i in range(N):
                for j in range(i + 1, N):
                    x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
                    x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
                    
                    # Circle 1: radius r1 = mid / c1
                    # Circle 2: radius r2 = mid / c2
                    r1 = mid / c1
                    r2 = mid / c2
                    
                    # Distance between centers
                    d_sq = distance_sq((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
                    
                    # No intersection if one circle is inside the other or they're too far
                    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
                        continue
                    
                    # Calculate intersection points
                    # Midpoint of the line joining centers
                    dx = x2 - x1
                    dy = y2 - y1
                    half_d = d / 2.0
                    
                    # Distance from center1 to the line joining the intersection points
                    # Using the formula: a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2*d)
                    a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
                    
                    # Height from the line to the intersection points
                    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - a*a)
                    
                    if h < 1e-10:  # Degenerate case (circles touch)
                        # Only one intersection point (or coincident)
                        # But we get one point (the midpoint)
                        point_x = x1 + a * dx / d
                        point_y = y1 + a * dy / d
                        count = 0
                        for k in range(N):
                            xk, yk, ck = meats[k]
                            dist = distance((point_x, point_y), (xk, yk))
                            radius_required = mid / ck
                            if dist <= radius_required:
                                count += 1
                        if count >= K:
                            feasible = True
                            break
                    else:
                        # Two intersection points
                        # The points are at (x1 + a*dx/d ± h*dy/d, y1 + a*dy/d ∓ h*dx/d)
                        # Actually: perpendicular to (dx,dy)
                        perp_x = -dy / d
                        perp_y = dx / d
                        
                        point1_x = x1 + a * dx / d + h * perp_x
                        point1_y = y1 + a * dy / d + h * perp_y
                        point2_x = x1 + a * dx / d - h * perp_x
                        point2_y = y1 + a * dy / d - h * perp_y
                        
                        # Check both points
                        for px, py in [(point1_x, point1_y), (point2_x, point2_y)]:
                            count = 0
                            for k in range(N):
                                xk, yk, ck = meats[k]
                                dist = distance((px, py), (xk, yk))
                                radius_required = mid / ck
                                if dist <= radius_required:
                                    count += 1
                            if count >= K:
                                feasible = True
                                break
                        if feasible:
                            break
                if feasible:
                    break
        
        if feasible:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.10f}")

solve()
```

This should work. Let me trace through the sample:
- N=4, K=3
- Meats: (-1,0,3), (0,0,3), (1,0,2), (1,1,40)

For T=2.4, we expect it to work.

For each piece, mid/c_i when mid=2.4:
- Piece 1: 2.4/3 = 0.8
- Piece 2: 2.4/3 = 0.8  
- Piece 3: 2.4/2 = 1.2
- Piece 4: 2.4/40 = 0.06

We need a point that is within these distances from at least 3 pieces.

The sample says placing at (-0.2, 0) works.

Distance from (-0.2,0) to pieces:
- Piece 1: sqrt((-0.2+1)^2 + (0-0)^2) = sqrt(0.64) = 0.8, time = 3*0.8 = 2.4
- Piece 2: sqrt((-0.2-0)^2) = 0.2, time = 3*0.2 = 0.6
- Piece 3: sqrt((-0.2-1)^2) = sqrt(1.44) = 1.2, time = 2*1.2 = 2.4
- Piece 4: sqrt((-0.2-1)^2 + (0-1)^2) = sqrt(1.44 + 1) = sqrt(2.44) ≈ 1.56, time = 40*1.56 ≈ 62.4

So pieces 1,2,3 are ready in 2.4 seconds.

In our algorithm, for T=2.4, we'll check if there's a point within 0.8 of piece1, 0.8 of piece2, 1.2 of piece3, 0.06 of piece4.

The point (-0.2,0) should be within:
- Distance to piece1: 0.8 ≤ 0.8 ✓
- Distance to piece2: 0.2 ≤ 0.8 ✓  
- Distance to piece3: 1.2 ≤ 1.2 ✓
- Distance to piece4: sqrt(1.2^2 + 1^2) = sqrt(1.44+1)=sqrt(2.44)≈1.56 > 0.06 ✗

But we only need 3 pieces. So it should be counted.

Our algorithm checks pairwise intersections and will eventually find such a point or its equivalent.

The point (-0.2,0) might not be a pairwise intersection of two circles, but it might be within distance of three pieces.

In our algorithm, we check:
- Centers: each center might be within distance of 1-2 pieces
- Pairwise intersections: we compute intersection points of pairs

The point (-0.2,0) isn't likely one of the pairwise intersections, but we have a flaw: we don't check all points.

But our "feasibility" check only happens at the intersection points and centers. We might miss points that are optimal but not at intersections.

For example, the point (-0.2,0) might not be an intersection of two circles.

However, note that the distance from (-0.2,0) to piece1 is exactly 0.8, which is the upper bound. If we consider the circle for piece1 with radius 0.8, (-0.2,0) is on the boundary.

Similarly, piece3 is at distance 1.2, on the boundary.

So (-0.2,0) is on the boundary of piece1 and piece3 circles.

But it's not the intersection point of the two circles.

The intersection point calculation would find points where distance to both piece1 and piece3 is exactly 0.8 and 1.2 respectively.

Let me calculate if (-0.2,0) satisfies both:
- Distance to piece1: sqrt(( -0.2 +1)^2) = 0.8 ✓
- Distance to piece3: sqrt(( -0.2 -1)^2) = sqrt(1.44) = 1.2 ✓

Yes, it satisfies both. Why isn't it found by our intersection algorithm?

For pieces 1 and 3:
- Piece1: center (-1,0), radius 0.8
- Piece3: center (1,0), radius 1.2
- Distance between centers = 2

Check: r1 + r2 = 0.8 + 1.2 = 2.0, so they touch externally.

When d = r1 + r2, there is exactly one intersection point at the midpoint.

The midpoint is at ((-1+1)/2, (0+0)/2) = (0,0)

Wait, that's (0,0), not (-0.2,0).

For circles (x+1)^2 + y^2 = 0.8^2 and (x-1)^2 + y^2 = 1.2^2:

First equation: (x+1)^2 + y^2 = 0.64
Second: (x-1)^2 + y^2 = 1.44

Subtract: (x-1)^2 - (x+1)^2 = 1.44 - 0.64
(x^2 - 2x + 1) - (x^2 + 2x + 1) = 0.8
-4x = 0.8
x = -0.2

Then from first equation: (-0.2 + 1)^2 + y^2 = 0.64
0.8^2 + y^2 = 0.64
0.64 + y^2 = 0.64
y^2 = 0
y = 0

So intersection point is (-0.2, 0)!

Yes! So our algorithm should find this point.

In our intersection point calculation for pieces 1 and 3 when T=2.4:
- d = 2.0
- r1 = 0.8, r2 = 1.2
- r1 + r2 = 2.0, so they touch
- a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2*d) = (0.64 - 1.44 + 4) / 4 = (3.2) / 4 = 0.8
- h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2) = sqrt(0.64 - 0.64) = 0
- So we have one point: x1 + a*(dx/d) = -1 + 0.8*(2/2) = -1 + 0.8 = -0.2
- y1 + a*(dy/d) = 0 + 0.8*(0/2) = 0

So point (-0.2, 0) is found.

Then we check how many pieces are within distance T/c_i from this point.

For piece1: dist = 0.8, required = 0.8, so 0.8 ≤ 0.8 ✓
For piece2: dist = sqrt((-0.2)^2) = 0.2, required = 0.8, so ✓
For piece3: dist = 1.2, required = 1.2, so ✓
For piece4: dist = sqrt(1.2^2 + 1^2) ≈ sqrt(1.44+1)=sqrt(2.44)≈1.56, required=0.06, so 1.56 > 0.06 ✗

So count = 3 ≥ K=3, so feasible.

Thus, the algorithm should work.

Final code:

```python
import math
import sys

def distance_sq(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt(distance_sq(p1, p2))

def main():
    # Read input
    line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    if not line:
        return
    N, K = map(int, line.split())
    
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, 300000.0
    eps = 1e-9
    
    while right - left > eps:
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check feasibility: is there a point that is within mid/c_i of at least K pieces?
        feasible = False
        
        # Check centers of pieces
        for i in range(N):
            center_x, center_y = meats[i][0], meats[i][1]
            count = 0
            for j in range(N):
                xj, yj, cj = meats[j]
                dist = distance((center_x, center_y), (xj, yj))
                radius_required = mid / cj
                if dist <= radius_required:
                    count += 1
            if count >= K:
                feasible = True
                break
        
        if not feasible:
            # Check pairwise intersection points of circles
            for i in range(N):
                for j in range(i + 1, N):
                    x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
                    x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
                    
                    # Circle 1: center (x1,y1), radius r1 = mid / c1
                    # Circle 2: center (x2,y2), radius r2 = mid / c2
                    r1 = mid / c1
                    r2 = mid / c2
                    
                    # Distance between centers
                    d_sq = distance_sq((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
                    
                    # No intersection if one circle is completely inside the other or they're too far
                    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
                        continue
                    
                    # Calculate intersection points
                    dx = x2 - x1
                    dy = y2 - y1
                    
                    # a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2*d)
                    a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
                    
                    # h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2)
                    h_sq = r1*r1 - a*a
                    if h_sq < 0:
                        continue
                    h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
                    
                    # The intersection points are:
                    # (x1 + a*dx/d ± h*dy/d, y1 + a*dy/d ∓ h*dx/d)
                    # But need to normalize (dx,dy)
                    perp_x = -dy / d
                    perp_y = dx / d
                    
                    # First intersection point
                    point1_x = x1 + a * (dx / d) + h * perp_x
                    point1_y = y1 + a * (dy / d) + h * perp_y
                    
                    # Second intersection point
                    point2_x = x1 + a * (dx / d) - h * perp_x
                    point2_y = y1 + a * (dy / d) - h * perp_y
                    
                    # Check both points
                    for px, py in [(point1_x, point1_y), (point2_x, point2_y)]:
                        count = 0
                        for k in range(N):
                            xk, yk, ck = meats[k]
                            dist = distance((px, py), (xk, yk))
                            radius_required = mid / ck
                            if dist <= radius_required:
                                count += 1
                        if count >= K:
                            feasible = True
                            break
                    if feasible:
                        break
                if feasible:
                    break
        
        if feasible:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.10f}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

import math
import sys

def distance_sq(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt(distance_sq(p1, p2))

def main():
    # Read input
    line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
    if not line:
        return
    N, K = map(int, line.split())
    
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, 300000.0
    eps = 1e-9
    
    while right - left > eps:
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check feasibility: is there a point that is within mid/c_i of at least K pieces?
        feasible = False

                    count += 1

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi wants to grill N pieces of meat on a grilling net, which can be seen as a two-dimensional plane. The coordinates of the i-th piece of meat are \left(x_i, y_i\right), and its hardness is c_i.
Takahashi can use one heat source to grill the meat. If he puts the heat source at coordinates \left(X, Y\right), where X and Y are real numbers, the i-th piece of meat will be ready to eat in c_i \times \sqrt{\left(X - x_i\right)^2 + \left(Y-y_i\right)^2} seconds.
Takahashi wants to eat K pieces of meat. Find the time required to have K or more pieces of meat ready if he put the heat source to minimize this time.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 60
 - 1 \leq K \leq N
 - -1000 \leq x_i , y_i \leq 1000
 - \left(x_i, y_i\right) \neq \left(x_j, y_j\right) \left(i \neq j \right)
 - 1 \leq c_i \leq 100

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
x_1 y_1 c_1
\vdots
x_N y_N c_N

-----Output-----
Print the answer.
It will be considered correct if its absolute or relative error from our answer is at most 10^{-6}.

-----Sample Input-----
4 3
-1 0 3
0 0 3
1 0 2
1 1 40

2.4

If we put the heat source at \left(-0.2, 0\right), the 1-st, 2-nd, and 3-rd pieces of meat will be ready to eat within 2.4 seconds. This is the optimal place to put the heat source.
