I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N pieces of meat at coordinates (x_i, y_i) with hardness c_i
2. We place a heat source at (X, Y) and the i-th piece is ready after c_i × distance seconds
3. We want to minimize the time when at least K pieces are ready
4. We need to find the optimal position (X, Y) for the heat source

## Key Insights

1. This is a geometric optimization problem - we need to find the optimal point (X,Y) that minimizes the maximum time to cook K pieces.
2. For a fixed time T, we can check if there's a point (X,Y) such that at least K pieces can be cooked within time T.
3. For piece i, if we want it to be ready in time T, we need: c_i × √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²) ≤ T
4. This means: √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²) ≤ T/c_i
5. Which means: (X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)² ≤ (T/c_i)²
6. So each piece i defines a circle centered at (x_i, y_i) with radius T/c_i
7. For a given T, if there's a point that lies within at least K such circles, then time T is achievable.

## Solution Approach

1. Use binary search on the answer (time T)
2. For each candidate time T, check if there exists a point (X,Y) that is inside at least K of the circles defined by each piece
3. To check if such a point exists, we can use the fact that if any K circles have a common intersection point, then T is achievable
4. However, finding the intersection of K circles is complex, so we can use a different approach:
   - For each subset of K pieces, check if their circles have a common intersection point
   - A point is in all K circles if it's in the intersection of the K circles
   - We can find the intersection points of pairs of circles and check if any of those points lies in the remaining K-2 circles

Actually, a simpler approach: since we want to minimize the maximum time to cook K pieces, we can use the fact that the optimal point will be such that at least K pieces are cooked in time T, and for each such piece, the distance constraint holds.

But the most practical approach given the constraints (N ≤ 60, K ≤ N) is to use binary search on time and for each time T, check if there's a point that is within distance T/c_i from at least K pieces.

To check this efficiently, we can note that if there's a point (X,Y) such that it's within distance T/c_i of at least K pieces, then the set of pieces that are within T of the point are at least K.

A key insight: if we consider the intersection of K circles, we can find if there's a common point by considering all possible combinations of K pieces and checking if their circles have a common intersection point.

However, there's a simpler observation: the optimal point will be such that it lies at the intersection of the boundaries of at least two circles (or is such that the time to cook K pieces is determined by the maximum of the c_i × distance). 

But given the constraints, a more practical approach is to use ternary search over the plane or use the fact that the optimal point often lies at some significant location like the intersection of two circles or at a point that minimizes the maximum distance.

Actually, let's use a different approach: binary search on time T, and for each T, check if there exists a point (X,Y) such that at least K pieces are within distance T/c_i of it.

To check this efficiently, we can:
1. For the given T, create a circle for each piece i with radius T/c_i
2. Check if there's a point that lies in at least K of these circles
3. This is equivalent to asking: does the intersection of at least K of these circles contain a point?

For small N, we can check all possible combinations of K pieces. For each combination of K pieces, determine if their corresponding circles have a common intersection point. If any such combination has a common intersection point, then T is achievable.

To find if K circles have a common intersection point:
- First check if the intersection of any 2 circles is non-empty and lies within all other circles
- If K=2, just check if the two circles intersect
- If K>2, check if there's a point that lies in all K circles

We can implement a function to find intersection points of two circles, then check if any such intersection point lies within all other circles.

## Implementation Steps

1. Read input
2. Binary search on time T from 0 to some upper bound (e.g., 10000)
3. For each T, check if there exists a point that is within distance T/c_i of at least K pieces
4. To check, consider all possible combinations of K pieces with the same T
5. For each combination, find if their circles have a common intersection point
6. If any combination has a common intersection point, T is achievable

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_squared(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def circles_intersect(c1, c2):
    # c1 = (center1, r1), c2 = (center2, r2)
    x1, y1 = c1[0], c1[1]
    x2, y2 = c2[0], c2[1]
    r1, r2 = c1[2], c2[2]
    
    d_sq = distance_squared((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
    
    # No intersection if circles are too far apart or one is inside the other
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False, None
    
    # Check if they intersect at exactly one point (tangent)
    if d == r1 + r2 or d == abs(r1 - r2):
        return True, None
    
    # They intersect at two points - need to find intersection points
    # The line connecting centers has length d
    # The intersection points lie on the perpendicular bisector at distance h from the line
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - ((d*d + r1*r1 - r2*r2) / (2*d))**2)
    
    # The midpoint of the line
    mx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    my = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    
    # Direction vector of the line
    dx = (x2 - x1) / d
    dy = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Perpendicular direction
    nx = -dy
    ny = dx
    
    # The intersection points
    px = mx + h * nx
    py = my + h * ny
    
    # And the symmetric point
    px2 = mx - h * nx
    py2 = my - h * ny
    
    return True, [(px, py), (px2, py2)]

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    dx = point[0] - center[0]
    dy = point[1] - center[1]
    return dx*dx + dy*dy <= radius*radius + 1e-9

def k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
    # circles: list of (x, y, r)
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each pair of circles, get their intersection points
    # Then check if any intersection point lies in all remaining circles
    if n == 2:
        return True
    
    # Try all intersection points from pairs
    # Since we have n circles, we need to check all pairs and see if their intersection point lies in all others
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            intersects, points = circles_intersect((circles[i][0], circles[i][1], circles[i][2]), 
                                                   (circles[j][0], circles[j][1], circles[j][2]))
            if intersects:
                for p in points:
                    # Check if point p lies within all other circles
                    valid = True
                    for idx in range(n):
                        if idx == i or idx == j:
                            continue
                        if not point_in_circle(p, circles[idx][:2], circles[idx][2]):
                            valid = False
                            break
                    if valid:
                        return True
    return False

def can_achieve_time(cases, k, time):
    # cases: list of (x, y, c)
    n = len(cases)
    if k == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each combination of k pieces, check if their circles have a common intersection
    for combo in combinations(range(n), k):
        # Create circles for each piece in the combination
        circles = []
        for i in combo:
            x, y, c = cases[i]
            r = time / c
            circles.append((x, y, r))
        
        if k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
            return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    cases = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        cases.append((x, y, c))
    
    # Binary search on time
    left, right = 0.0, 10000.0
    
    # Do binary search until we get precise enough result
    for _ in range(100):  # 100 iterations should give enough precision
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if can_achieve_time(cases, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

solve()
```

Wait, there's an issue with the `circles_intersect` function. The logic for finding intersection points is correct, but there's a calculation error. Let me fix it:

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_squared(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def circles_intersect(c1, c2):
    # c1 = (center1, r1), c2 = (center2, r2)
    x1, y1 = c1[0], c1[1]
    x2, y2 = c2[0], c2[1]
    r1, r2 = c1[2], c2[2]
    
    d_sq = distance_squared((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
    
    # No intersection if circles are too far apart or one is inside the other
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False, None
    
    # Check if they intersect at exactly one point (tangent)
    if d == r1 + r2 or d == abs(r1 - r2):
        # Return one point for tangent case
        # For d = r1 + r2: point is on the extension of the line
        # For d = |r1 - r2|: point is on the line segment
        if d == r1 + r2:
            # The intersection point is at the boundary
            dx = (x2 - x1) / d
            dy = (y2 - y1) / d
            px = x1 + r1 * dx
            py = y1 + r1 * dy
            return True, [(px, py)]
        else:  # d == abs(r1 - r2)
            # The intersection point is at the boundary
            dx = (x2 - x1) / d
            dy = (y2 - y1) / d
            if r1 >= r2:
                px = x1 + (r1 - r2) * dx
                py = y1 + (r1 - r2) * dy
            else:
                px = x1 + (r2 - r1) * dx
                py = y1 + (r2 - r1) * dy
            return True, [(px, py)]
    
    # General case: two intersection points
    # The line connecting centers has length d
    # The intersection points lie on the perpendicular bisector at distance h from the line
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - ((d*d + r1*r1 - r2*r2) / (2*d))**2)
    
    # The midpoint of the line
    mx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    my = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    
    # Direction vector of the line
    dx = (x2 - x1) / d
    dy = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Perpendicular direction
    nx = -dy
    ny = dx
    
    # The intersection points
    px1 = mx + h * nx
    py1 = my + h * ny
    px2 = mx - h * nx
    py2 = my - h * ny
    
    return True, [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    dx = point[0] - center[0]
    dy = point[1] - center[1]
    return dx*dx + dy*dy <= radius*radius + 1e-9

def k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
    # circles: list of (x, y, r)
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each pair of circles, get their intersection points
    # Then check if any intersection point lies in all remaining circles
    if n == 2:
        return True
    
    # Try all intersection points from pairs
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            intersects, points = circles_intersect((circles[i][0], circles[i][1], circles[i][2]), 
                                                   (circles[j][0], circles[j][1], circles[j][2]))
            if intersects:
                for p in points:
                    # Check if point p lies within all other circles
                    valid = True
                    for idx in range(n):
                        if idx == i or idx == j:
                            continue
                        if not point_in_circle(p, circles[idx][:2], circles[idx][2]):
                            valid = False
                            break
                    if valid:
                        return True
    return False

def can_achieve_time(cases, k, time):
    # cases: list of (x, y, c)
    n = len(cases)
    if k == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each combination of k pieces, check if their circles have a common intersection
    for combo in combinations(range(n), k):
        # Create circles for each piece in the combination
        circles = []
        for i in combo:
            x, y, c = cases[i]
            r = time / c
            circles.append((x, y, r))
        
        if k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
            return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    cases = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        cases.append((x, y, c))
    
    # Binary search on time
    left, right = 0.0, 10000.0
    
    # Do binary search until we get precise enough result
    for _ in range(100):  # 100 iterations should give enough precision
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if can_achieve_time(cases, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

solve()
```

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_squared(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def circles_intersect(c1, c2):
    # c1 = (center1, r1), c2 = (center2, r2)
    x1, y1 = c1[0], c1[1]
    x2, y2 = c2[0], c2[1]
    r1, r2 = c1[2], c2[2]
    
    d_sq = distance_squared((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
    
    # No intersection if circles are too far apart or one is inside the other
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False, None
    
    # For tangent cases, we have exactly one intersection point
    if d == r1 + r2 or d == abs(r1 - r2):
        # Calculate the intersection point
        if d == 0:
            # Same center, circles overlap if r1 == r2
            if abs(r1 - r2) < 1e-9:
                return True, [(x1, y1)]
            else:
                return False, None
        
        # For d = r1 + r2 (externally tangent)
        dx = (x2 - x1) / d
        dy = (y2 - y1) / d
        px = x1 + r1 * dx
        py = y1 + r1 * dy
        return True, [(px, py)]
    
    # General case: two intersection points
    # The line connecting centers has length d
    # The intersection points lie on the perpendicular bisector at distance h from the line
    # Using the formula: h = sqrt(r1^2 - ((d^2 + r1^2 - r2^2)/(2d))^2)
    numerator = d*d + r1*r1 - r2*r2
    if abs(numerator) < 1e-12:
        # Special case when circles are symmetric
        h = 0
    else:
        h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - ((numerator / (2*d))**2))
    
    # The midpoint of the line
    mx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    my = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    
    # Direction vector of the line
    dx = (x2 - x1) / d
    dy = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Perpendicular direction
    nx = -dy
    ny = dx
    
    # The intersection points
    px1 = mx + h * nx
    py1 = my + h * ny
    px2 = mx - h * nx
    py2 = my - h * ny
    
    return True, [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    dx = point[0] - center[0]
    dy = point[1] - center[1]
    return dx*dx + dy*dy <= radius*radius + 1e-9

def k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
    # circles: list of (x, y, r)
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each pair of circles, get their intersection points
    # Then check if any intersection point lies in all remaining circles
    if n == 2:
        return True
    
    # Try all intersection points from pairs
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            intersects, points = circles_intersect((circles[i][0], circles[i][1], circles[i][2]), 
                                                   (circles[j][0], circles[j][1], circles[j][2]))
            if intersects:
                for p in points:
                    # Check if point p lies within all other circles
                    valid = True
                    for idx in range(n):
                        if idx == i or idx == j:
                            continue
                        if not point_in_circle(p, circles[idx][:2], circles[idx][2]):
                            valid = False
                            break
                    if valid:
                        return True
    return False

def can_achieve_time(cases, k, time):
    # cases: list of (x, y, c)
    n = len(cases)
    if k == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each combination of k pieces, check if their circles have a common intersection
    for combo in combinations(range(n), k):
        # Create circles for each piece in the combination
        circles = []
        for i in combo:
            x, y, c = cases[i]
            r = time / c
            circles.append((x, y, r))
        
        if k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
            return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    cases = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
```

import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_squared(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def circles_intersect(c1, c2):
    # c1 = (center1, r1), c2 = (center2, r2)
    x1, y1 = c1[0], c1[1]
    x2, y2 = c2[0], c2[1]
    r1, r2 = c1[2], c2[2]
    
    d_sq = distance_squared((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
    
    # No intersection if circles are too far apart or one is inside the other
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return False, None
    
    # For tangent cases, we have exactly one intersection point
    if d == r1 + r2 or d == abs(r1 - r2):
        # Calculate the intersection point
        if d == 0:
            # Same center, circles overlap if r1 == r2
            if abs(r1 - r2) < 1e-9:
                return True, [(x1, y1)]
            else:
                return False, None
        
        # For d = r1 + r2 (externally tangent)
        dx = (x2 - x1) / d
        dy = (y2 - y1) / d
        px = x1 + r1 * dx
        py = y1 + r1 * dy
        return True, [(px, py)]
    
    # General case: two intersection points
    # The line connecting centers has length d
    # The intersection points lie on the perpendicular bisector at distance h from the line
    # Using the formula: h = sqrt(r1^2 - ((d^2 + r1^2 - r2^2)/(2d))^2)
    numerator = d*d + r1*r1 - r2*r2
    if abs(numerator) < 1e-12:
        # Special case when circles are symmetric
        h = 0
    else:
        h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - ((numerator / (2*d))**2))
    
    # The midpoint of the line
    mx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    my = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    
    # Direction vector of the line
    dx = (x2 - x1) / d
    dy = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Perpendicular direction
    nx = -dy
    ny = dx
    
    # The intersection points
    px1 = mx + h * nx
    py1 = my + h * ny
    px2 = mx - h * nx
    py2 = my - h * ny
    
    return True, [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    dx = point[0] - center[0]
    dy = point[1] - center[1]
    return dx*dx + dy*dy <= radius*radius + 1e-9

def k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
    # circles: list of (x, y, r)
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each pair of circles, get their intersection points
    # Then check if any intersection point lies in all remaining circles
    if n == 2:
        return True
    
    # Try all intersection points from pairs
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            intersects, points = circles_intersect((circles[i][0], circles[i][1], circles[i][2]), 
                                                   (circles[j][0], circles[j][1], circles[j][2]))
            if intersects:
                for p in points:
                    # Check if point p lies within all other circles
                    valid = True
                    for idx in range(n):
                        if idx == i or idx == j:
                            continue
                        if not point_in_circle(p, circles[idx][:2], circles[idx][2]):
                            valid = False
                            break
                    if valid:
                        return True
    return False

def can_achieve_time(cases, k, time):
    # cases: list of (x, y, c)
    n = len(cases)
    if k == 1:
        return True
    
    # For each combination of k pieces, check if their circles have a common intersection
    for combo in combinations(range(n), k):
        # Create circles for each piece in the combination
        circles = []
        for i in combo:
            x, y, c = cases[i]
            r = time / c
            circles.append((x, y, r))
        
        if k_circles_have_common_intersection(circles):
            return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    cases = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        cases.append((x, y, c))

    for _ in range(100):  # 100 iterations should give enough precision

        if can_achieve_time(cases, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid

Takahashi wants to grill N pieces of meat on a grilling net, which can be seen as a two-dimensional plane. The coordinates of the i-th piece of meat are \left(x_i, y_i\right), and its hardness is c_i.
Takahashi can use one heat source to grill the meat. If he puts the heat source at coordinates \left(X, Y\right), where X and Y are real numbers, the i-th piece of meat will be ready to eat in c_i \times \sqrt{\left(X - x_i\right)^2 + \left(Y-y_i\right)^2} seconds.
Takahashi wants to eat K pieces of meat. Find the time required to have K or more pieces of meat ready if he put the heat source to minimize this time.

-----Constraints-----

 - 1 \leq N \leq 60
 - 1 \leq K \leq N
 - -1000 \leq x_i , y_i \leq 1000
 - \left(x_i, y_i\right) \neq \left(x_j, y_j\right) \left(i \neq j \right)
 - 1 \leq c_i \leq 100

Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
x_1 y_1 c_1
\vdots
x_N y_N c_N

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N pieces of meat at coordinates (x_i, y_i) with hardness c_i
2. We place a heat source at (X, Y) and the i-th piece takes c_i × distance seconds to cook
3. We want to find the minimum time such that at least K pieces are ready (cooked within that time)
4. We need to minimize this time by optimally placing the heat source

## Key Insights

1. This is a classic "minimum time to cook K pieces" problem with geometric optimization
2. For a fixed time T, we can determine which pieces can be cooked within T seconds by checking if c_i × distance ≤ T
3. Distance from heat source (X,Y) to piece i is √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²)
4. So we need c_i × √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²) ≤ T, which means √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²) ≤ T/c_i
5. This means the point (X,Y) must be within distance T/c_i from (x_i, y_i)

Let me implement the circle intersection.

Circle 1: center (x1,y1), radius r1 = T / c1
Circle 2: center (x2,y2), radius r2 = T / c2

Let dx = x2 - x1, dy = y2 - y1, d = sqrt(dx^2 + dy^2)

If d > r1 + r2 or d < |r1 - r2|, no intersection.

If d == 0 and r1 == r2, infinite solutions (they are the same circle)

If d == 0 and r1 != r2, no intersection.

Otherwise, the line joining centers has direction (dx, dy). We can find the intersection points.

The distance from center1 to the line joining intersection points is h = sqrt(r1^2 - (d^2 - (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2*d))^2) - actually standard formula:

The intersection points can be found by:

Let a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2*d)
Let h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2)

Then the point on the line at distance a from center1 is:
x = x1 + a * dx / d
y = y1 + a * dy / d

Then the two intersection points are:
(x + h * dy / d, y - h * dx / d)
(x - h * dy / d, y + h * dx / d)

This is the standard formula.

We need to be careful with floating point precision.

Also, we need to check if h is valid (h >= 0).

Let me code this.

Also, we should include the centers of each piece as candidate points.

Actually, at center (x_i, y_i), the cooking time for piece i is 0, for others it's c_j * distance.

So we can check all centers.

But centers might not be optimal, but they are valid candidates.

Final algorithm:

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_sq(x1, y1, x2, y2):
    return (x1 - x2) ** 2 + (y1 - y2) ** 2

def solve():
    # Read input
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If K == 1, we can just place at any piece, time = 0
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    # Upper bound: worst case - place heat source very far, but actually we can use maximum possible time
    # Upper bound: place at one point, worst cooking time for any piece is c_i * max_distance
    # max_distance is at most 2000*2 = 4000, max c_i is 100, so upper bound ~ 400000
    # But actually, if we place at a point, worst case is when we only cook one piece, but we might cook multiple
    # We can set upper bound to 400000
    
    left, right = 0.0, 400000.0
    
    # We need precision of 1e-6
    for _ in range(100):  # binary search for 100 iterations gives precision about 1e-30
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if feasible(meats, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

def feasible(meats, K, T):
    # T is the time we want to achieve
    # Check if there exists a point (X,Y) such that at least K pieces have cooking time <= T
    
    # First, check all centers of pieces
    x_coords = [m[0] for m in meats]
    y_coords = [m[1] for m in meats]
    c_coords = [m[2] for m in meats]
    
    # Check each center
    for i in range(len(meats)):
        x, y, c = meats[i]
        # At point (x,y), calculate cooking time for each piece
        times = []
        for j in range(len(meats)):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[j]
            dist = math.sqrt((x - x1)**2 + (y - y1)**2)
            times.append(c1 * dist)
        
        if sum(1 for t in times if t <= T) >= K:
            return True
    
    # Check intersection points of pairs of circles
    n = len(meats)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
            
            # Radius for piece i: T/c1, for piece j: T/c2
            r1 = T / c1
            r2 = T / c2
            
            # Distance between centers
            dx = x2 - x1
            dy = y2 - y1
            d = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
            
            # No intersection if circles are too far or too close
            if d == 0:
                # Same center - only if r1 == r2, which means T/c1 == T/c2 -> c1 == c2
                if r1 == r2:
                    # All pieces at same center have same distance, but we only know about i and j
                    # At this center, check if at least K pieces have time <= T
                    times = []
                    for k in range(n):
                        x3, y3, c3 = meats[k]
                        dist = math.sqrt((x1 - x3)**2 + (y1 - y3)**2)
                        times.append(c3 * dist)
                    if sum(1 for t in times if t <= T) >= K:
                        return True
                continue
            
            # Check if intersection exists
            # |r1 - r2| <= d <= r1 + r2
            if d < abs(r1 - r2) or d > r1 + r2:
                continue
            
            # Calculate intersection points
            # a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2 * d)
            a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2.0 * d)
            
            # h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2)
            h_sq = r1*r1 - a*a
            if h_sq < 0:
                continue
            h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
            
            # Point on line joining centers at distance a from (x1,y1)
            x_mid = x1 + a * dx / d
            y_mid = y1 + a * dy / d
            
            # Two intersection points
            # Using: (x, y) = (x_mid ± h * dy/d, y_mid ∓ h * dx/d)
            # The formula is: (x_mid ± h * dy/d, y_mid ∓ h * dx/d)
            # But this gives correct for both points?
            # Yes, because the vector (dy/d, -dx/d) is perpendicular to (dx,dy)
            
            points = [
                (x_mid + h * dy / d, y_mid - h * dx / d),
                (x_mid - h * dy / d, y_mid + h * dx / d)
            ]
            
            for x, y in points:
                # Check cooking time for all pieces at this point
                times = []
                for k in range(len(meats)):
                    x3, y3, c3 = meats[k]
                    dist = math.sqrt((x - x3)**2 + (y - y3)**2)
                    times.append(c3 * dist)
                
                if sum(1 for t in times if t <= T) >= K:
                    return True
    
    return False

solve()
```

Wait, I see an issue: if d == 0, we handled it, but what if r1 == 0? Actually, T ≥ 0, c1 ≥ 1, so r1 = T/c1 ≥ 0, and if T=0, then r1=0.

Also, in the case where d==0 and r1==r2, we have a common circle, and we check the center.

But there's also the possibility that T is very large, so many pieces can be cooked.

Now, is (-0.2,0) covered in our pairs?

|0.8-0.8|=0 ≤ 1.0 ≤ 1.6 ✓

a = (0.8^2 - 0.8^2 + 1.0^2)/(2*1.0) = 1.0/2 = 0.5
h_sq = 0.8^2 - 0.5^2 = 0.39, h=√0.39≈0.6245

x_mid = -1 + 0.5*(1)/1 = -0.5
y_mid = 0

Points: (-0.5 ± 0, -0.6245) and (-0.5 ± 0, 0.6245)? 

Actually, (x_mid + h * dy/d, y_mid - h * dx/d)
dx=1, dy=0, d=1
Point1: (-0.5 + 0.6245*0/1, 0 - 0.6245*1/1) = (-0.5, -0.6245)
Point2: (-0.5 - 0.6245*0/1, 0 + 0.6245*1/1) = (-0.5, 0.6245)

So our intersection points are not on the x-axis. 

What's the issue? 

The formula: the two points are:
(x_mid + h * (dy/d), y_mid - h * (dx/d))
(x_mid - h * (dy/d), y_mid + h * (dx/d))

With dx=1, dy=0, d=1:
Point1: (-0.5 + h*0, 0 - h*1) = (-0.5, -h)
Point2: (-0.5 - 0, 0 + h) = (-0.5, h)

But (-0.2,0) is not among them.

Is (-0.2,0) on the circles?

Distance from (-1,0) to (-0.2,0): 0.8 → 0.8^2 = 0.64, and 0.8^2 = 0.64, so yes.
Distance from (0,0) to (-0.2,0): 0.2 → 0.2^2 = 0.04, but radius is 0.8, 0.2^2=0.04 ≠ 0.64 → no.

For piece 2, the radius is T/c2 = 2.4/3 = 0.8, so distance should be ≤ 0.8, and 0.2 ≤ 0.8, so it is within.

The circle of piece 2 has radius 0.8, so any point within distance 0.8 from (0,0) is in it.

(-0.2,0) is at distance 0.2 from (0,0), which is < 0.8, so it is in the circle.

But for the intersection of two circles of radius 0.8 centered at (-1,0) and (0,0), the points satisfy being within 0.8 of both.

(-0.2,0): distance to (-1,0) is 0.8, so on boundary ✓
distance to (0,0) is 0.2 < 0.8 ✓

So it should be in the intersection.

But according to our calculation of intersection points, we only got points at (-0.5, ±0.6245)

Let me verify one of them:
For (-0.5, 0.6245):
distance to (-1,0): √(0.5^2 + 0.6245^2) = √(0.25+0.39) = √0.64 = 0.8 ✓
distance to (0,0): √(0.5^2 + 0.6245^2) = same = 0.8 ✓

So both points are on both circles.

But (-0.2,0) is also within both circles? Distance to (-1,0) is 0.8, to (0,0) is 0.2, both ≤ 0.8.

But it's not on the boundary of both. It's inside.

So our algorithm only checks intersection points (boundary), not interior points.

We need to check if there's a point in the intersection of the two circles that is in the intersection of K circles.

The intersection of two circles has a region, not just points.

So our method is flawed.

We need a different approach.

Alternative idea: for a fixed T, the set of feasible points is the intersection of the K circles (each with radius T/c_i center (x_i,y_i)).

We need to check if this intersection is non-empty.

The intersection of multiple circles can be checked by checking if there's a point in all of them.

Since the region is convex (intersection of disks), we can check if it's non-empty by checking if the intersection of the K circles is non-empty.

We can do this by checking if there's a point in the intersection by solving a system.

But a practical way is to note that if the intersection of K circles is non-empty, then there exists a point that is within distance T/c_i from (x_i,y_i) for each of the K pieces.

One way is to use a geometric median or iterative method, but it's complex.

Another idea: since the function is convex, the minimum time is achieved when the heat source is at a point where the cooking times for the K pieces are equal, or at a boundary.

But I think for the purpose of this problem, and given the small N, we can use the fact that the optimal point is at the intersection of the boundaries of two or more pieces.

But as we saw, (-0-2,0) is not at the intersection of boundaries of the two circles (for piece 1 and 2), it is in the interior.

For piece 1 and 2, both have radius 0.8, and (-0.2,0) is within both.

The intersection of two circles is a lens-shaped region, and we can check if a point is in it.

To check if the intersection of K circles is non-empty, we can use the fact that if for every pair, the distance between centers is ≤ sum of radii, and for every triple, etc., but it's complex.

A simpler approach for small N: since N is only 60, but in practice the number of pieces that can be served is K, and K can be up to 60, we might need a better method.

I found that in competitive programming, this problem is sometimes solved by binary search on T and then using a geometric sweep or trying all pairs as candidates.

But I think for this platform, and given the sample, maybe the intended solution is to try a different type of candidate.

Perhaps the only candidate points are the centers and the intersection points of pairs of boundaries.

But as we've seen, (-0.2,0) is not one of them.

Unless we try a different pair.

What if we try piece 1 and piece 3?

Piece 1: c1=3, T=2.4, r1=0.8
Piece 3: c3=2, T=2.4, r3=1.2
Centers: (-1,0) and (1,0), distance = 2.0
|0.8-1.2|=0.4 ≤ 2.0 ≤ 2.0 ✓ (exactly at boundary)

a = (0.8^2 - 1.2^2 + 2.0^2) / (2*2.0) = (0.64 - 1.44 + 4.0)/4 = 3.2/4 = 0.8
h_sq = 0.8^2 - 0.8^2 = 0, h=0
x_mid = -1 + 0.8*(2)/2 = -1 + 0.8 = -0.2
y_mid = 0
Points: (-0.2, 0) and (-0.2, 0) (same point)

Yes! So at (-0.2,0), we have the intersection point.

Then we can check at (-0.2,0) if at least K=3 pieces have time ≤ 2.4.

As calculated earlier: pieces 1,2,3 have time 2.4, which is ≤ 2.4, and piece 4 has time >2.4, so 3 pieces.

So it works.

Therefore, by including all pairs of pieces, and checking their intersection points, we do cover the point (-0.2,0) because it is the intersection point of the boundaries of piece 1 and piece 3.

So our algorithm should work.

Now, in our code, for each pair, we generate the intersection points (which may be one or two points), and for each, we check if at least K pieces have cooking time ≤ T.

In the case where the circles are tangent, we get one point.

In the case where they intersect at two points, we get two points.

We also need to handle when d is very small or zero.

In the code above, when d==0, we only consider if r1==r2, and then we use that center.

But when d==0 and r1==r2, the circles are the same, so any point in the circle will work.

In that case, we can use any point in the circle, for example the center.

But in our current code, we only check if at the center, at least K pieces have time ≤ T.

But if we have a circle of radius r with center (x,y), then any point in the circle is within distance r from (x,y), so for that piece, cooking time is c_i * distance ≤ c_i * r.

For other pieces, it might be different.

But if we have many pieces with the same center, and we place at that center, it might not be optimal.

However, in the case where d==0 and r1==r2, the circles are identical, so any point in the circle will be within r from both centers.

But since the centers are the same, the condition for each piece is that distance to (x,y) ≤ T/c_i.

For a point in the circle, distance to (x,y) is ≤ r = T/c_i only if c_i * distance ≤ T.

Since distance ≤ r = T/c_i, then c_i * distance ≤ c_i * (T/c_i) = T.

So for any point in the circle, the cooking time for each piece is ≤ T.

Therefore, if we have multiple pieces at the same center, placing at that center will cook all of them within time T.

In our code, we are already checking the center point, and if at that center, at least K pieces have time ≤ T, then we return True.

For a point in the interior, we might have even better, but since we are only checking the center, and if at the center, the condition is met, then it is sufficient.

For the intersection of two circles with the same center, we have the same center, and we check at that center.

For a point in the intersection, it might be that for a piece, the distance is less than r, so time < T, which is still ≤ T.

So if at the center, the condition is met for at least K pieces, then a point in the intersection will also meet the condition (since distances are smaller or equal).

Therefore, it is sufficient to check the center point when the centers are the same.

In our code, we only check the center once, and if at that center, at least K pieces have time ≤ T, we return True.

For a different point in the intersection, we don't need to check because if the center works, then any point in the circle will work, and in particular, for the pieces at that center, the time will be ≤ T.

But for pieces not at that center, the time might be larger.

However, if at the center, for at least K pieces, the time is ≤ T, then at any point in the circle, for those K pieces, the distance to their centers is ≤ distance to the center (since it's a circle), so time ≤ c_i * distance to center ≤ c_i * (T/c_i) = T.

Therefore, those K pieces will still be cooked within time T.

So in that case, the condition is satisfied.

Therefore, our method of checking the center is sufficient for the case where centers are the same.

For the case where centers are different, we generate the intersection points and check them.

Therefore, the code should work.

Let me revise the code to ensure it handles all cases.

Final code:

```python
import math

def distance_sq(x1, y1, x2, y2):
    return (x1 - x2) ** 2 + (y1 - y2) ** 2

def solve():
    # Read input
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If K == 1, answer is 0
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, 400000.0
    
    # We need high precision
    for _ in range(100):
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if feasible(meats, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

def feasible(meats, K, T):
    n = len(meats)
    
    # Check all centers of pieces
    for i in range(n):
        x, y, c = meats[i]
        # Calculate cooking time for each piece at (x,y)
        count = 0
        for j in range(n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[j]
            dist = math.sqrt((x - x1) ** 2 + (y - y1) ** 2)
            if c1 * dist <= T:
                count += 1
        if count >= K:
            return True
    
    # Check intersection points of pairs of circles
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
            
            # Radius for piece i and piece j
            r1 = T / c1
            r2 = T / c2
            
            # Distance between centers
            dx = x2 - x1
            dy = y2 - y1
            d = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
            
            # Skip if distance is 0 or if no intersection
            if d == 0:
                # If both have same radius, then any point in the circle works
                if abs(r1 - r2) < 1e-9:  # practically equal
                    # Check if at the center, at least K pieces have time <= T
                    # But we already checked centers above
                    # So we don't need to check here
                    # Actually, if the centers are the same, and we have the same radius, then any point in the circle works
                    # So at the center, if at least K pieces have time <= T, we would have caught it
                    # So we can skip
                    continue
                else:
                    continue
            elif d < abs(r1 - r2) or d > r1 + r2:
                continue
            
            # Calculate the intersection points
            # a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2 * d)
            a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2.0 * d)
            
            # h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2)
            h_sq = r1*r1 - a*a
            if h_sq < 0:
                continue
            h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
            
            # Point on the line at distance a from (x1,y1)
            x_mid = x1 + a * dx / d
            y_mid = y1 + a * dy / d
            
            # Two intersection points
            # (x_mid + h * dy/d, y_mid - h * dx/d)
            # (x_mid - h * dy/d, y_mid + h * dx/d)
            point1 = (x_mid + h * dy / d, y_mid - h * dx / d)
            point2 = (x_mid - h * dy / d, y_mid + h * dx / d)
            
            # Check each point
            for x, y in [point1, point2]:
                count = 0
                for k in range(n):
                    x1, y1, c1 = meats[k]
                    dist = math.sqrt((x - x1) ** 2 + (y - y1) ** 2)
                    if c1 * dist <= T:
                        count += 1
                if count >= K:
                    return True
    
    return False

solve()
```

This should work for the sample and other cases.

Let me run through the sample with T=2.4 and the pair (0,0) and (1,0) to see if it catches (-0.2,0).

For pair (1,0) and (1,1): centers (1,0) and (1,1), d=1, r1=2.4/2=1.2, r2=2.4/40=0.06
|1.2-0.06|=1.14 > 1, so no intersection.

For pair (-1,0) and (1,0): d=2, r1=0.8, r2=1.2, |0.8-1.2|=0.4 ≤ 2 ≤ 2.0 ✓
a = (0.8^2 - 1.2^2 + 2^2) / (2*2) = (0.64 - 1.44 + 4) / 4 = 3.2/4 = 0.8
h_sq = 0.8^2 - 0.8^2 = 0, h=0
x_mid = -1 + 0.8 * (2)/2 = -1 + 0.8 = -0.2
y_mid = 0
Points: (-0.2,0) and (-0.2,0) (same)

Then at (-0.2,0), we check the times:
- p1: 3 * sqrt(0.8^2) = 3*0.8 = 2.4 ≤ 2.4 ✓
- p2: 3 * sqrt(0.2^2) = 3*0.2 = 0.6 ≤ 2.4 ✓
- p3: 2 * sqrt(1.2^2) = 2*1.2 = 2.4 ≤ 2.4 ✓
- p4: 40 * sqrt(1.2^2+1^2) = 40 * sqrt(1.44+1) = 40*sqrt(2.44)≈40*1.562≈62.4 >2.4 ✗
Count = 3 ≥ 3, so returns True.

Therefore, it works.

I will submit this code.

Note: The upper bound 400000 might be too large, but it's safe given the constraints.

We do 100 iterations of binary search, which gives sufficient precision.

We might want to use a smaller upper bound, but in worst case, if we place at a far point, the time might be large.

The maximum distance between any two points is at most 2000*2=4000, and max c_i is 100, so maximum time for a piece is 100*4000=400000, so upper bound is safe.

Final code:

```python
import math

def solve():
    # Read input
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If K == 1, answer is 0
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, 400000.0
    
    # We need high precision
    for _ in range(100):
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if feasible(meats, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

def feasible(meats, K, T):
    n = len(meats)
    
    # Check all centers of pieces
    for i in range(n):
        x, y, c = meats[i]
        count = 0
        for j in range(n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[j]
            dist = math.sqrt((x - x1) ** 2 + (y - y1) ** 2)
            if c1 * dist <= T:
                count += 1
        if count >= K:
            return True
    
    # Check intersection points of pairs of circles
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
            
            # Radius for piece i and piece j
            r1 = T / c1
            r2 = T / c2
            
            # Distance between centers
            dx = x2 - x1
            dy = y2 - y1
            d = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
            
            # Skip if distance is 0 or if no intersection
            if d < 1e-12:  # essentially 0
                # If both have same radius, then any point in the circle works
                # But we already checked centers
                continue
            elif d < abs(r1 - r2) or d > r1 + r2:
                continue
            
            # Calculate the intersection points
            # a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2 * d)
            a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2.0 * d)
            
            # h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2)
            h_sq = r1*r1 - a*a
            if h_sq < 0:
                continue
            h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
            
            # Point on the line at distance a from (x1,y1)
            x_mid = x1 + a * dx / d
            y_mid = y1 + a * dy / d
            
            # Two intersection points
            point1 = (x_mid + h * dy / d, y_mid - h * dx / d)
            point2 = (x_mid - h * dy / d, y_mid + h * dx / d)
            
            # Check each point
            for x, y in [point1, point2]:
                count = 0
                for k in range(n):
                    x1, y1, c1 = meats[k]
                    dist = math.sqrt((x - x1) ** 2 + (y - y1) ** 2)
                    if c1 * dist <= T:
                        count += 1
                if count >= K:
                    return True
    
    return False

solve()
```

import math

def solve():
    # Read input
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    meats = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If K == 1, answer is 0
    if K == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, 400000.0
    
    # We need high precision
    for _ in range(100):
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if feasible(meats, K, mid):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

def feasible(meats, K, T):
    n = len(meats)
    
    # Check all centers of pieces
    for i in range(n):
        x, y, c = meats[i]
        count = 0
        for j in range(n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[j]
            dist = math.sqrt((x - x1) ** 2 + (y - y1) ** 2)
            if c1 * dist <= T:
                count += 1
        if count >= K:
            return True
    
    # Check intersection points of pairs of circles
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
            
            # Radius for piece i and piece j
            r1 = T / c1
            r2 = T / c2
            
            # Distance between centers
            dx = x2 - x1
            dy = y2 - y1
            d = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
            
            # Skip if distance is 0 or if no intersection
            if d < 1e-12:  # essentially 0
                # If both have same radius, then any point in the circle works
                # But we already checked centers
                continue
            elif d < abs(r1 - r2) or d > r1 + r2:
                continue
            
            # Calculate the intersection points
            # a = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d^2) / (2 * d)
            a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2.0 * d)
            
            # h = sqrt(r1^2 - a^2)
            h_sq = r1*r1 - a*a
            if h_sq < 0:
                continue
            h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
            
            # Point on the line at distance a from (x1,y1)
            x_mid = x1 + a * dx / d
            y_mid = y1 + a * dy / d
            
            # Two intersection points
            point1 = (x_mid + h * dy / d, y_mid - h * dx / d)
            point2 = (x_mid - h * dy / d, y_mid + h * dx / d)
            
            # Check each point
            for x, y in [point1, point2]:
                count = 0
                for k in range(n):
                    x1, y1, c1 = meats[k]
                    dist = math.sqrt((x - x1) ** 2 + (y - y1) ** 2)
                    if c1 * dist <= T:
                        count += 1
                if count >= K:
                    return True
    
    return False

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi wants to grill N pieces of meat on a grilling net, which can be seen as a two-dimensional plane. The coordinates of the i-th piece of meat are \left(x_i, y_i\right), and its hardness is c_i.
Takahashi can use one heat source to grill the meat. If he puts the heat source at coordinates \left(X, Y\right), where X and Y are real numbers, the i-th piece of meat will be ready to eat in c_i \times \sqrt{\left(X - x_i\right)^2 + \left(Y-y_i\right)^2} seconds.
Takahashi wants to eat K pieces of meat. Find the time required to have K or more pieces of meat ready if he put the heat source to minimize this time.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 60
 - 1 \leq K \leq N
 - -1000 \leq x_i , y_i \leq 1000
 - \left(x_i, y_i\right) \neq \left(x_j, y_j\right) \left(i \neq j \right)
 - 1 \leq c_i \leq 100

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
x_1 y_1 c_1
\vdots
x_N y_N c_N

-----Output-----
Print the answer.
It will be considered correct if its absolute or relative error from our answer is at most 10^{-6}.

-----Sample Input-----
4 3
-1 0 3
0 0 3
1 0 2
1 1 40

-----Sample Output-----
2.4

If we put the heat source at \left(-0.2, 0\right), the 1-st, 2-nd, and 3-rd pieces of meat will be ready to eat within 2.4 seconds. This is the optimal place to put the heat source.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N pieces of meat at positions (x_i, y_i) with hardness c_i
2. We place a heat source at (X, Y) and the i-th piece takes c_i × distance(time) to cook
3. We want to minimize the time when at least K pieces are ready
4. The time for piece i is c_i × √((X-x_i)² + (Y-y_i)²)

2. The time when K pieces are ready is the maximum of the cooking times for the K pieces that finish first.
3. This is a classic "minimize the maximum" problem, which can be solved using binary search on the answer.

Let me implement this candidate point approach.

Steps:
1. Generate all candidate points:
   - All (x_i, y_i) for i in range(N)
   - All intersection points of pairs of circles
2. For each candidate point, compute the time when each piece is ready (c_i × distance)
3. For each candidate point, find the time T such that at least K pieces have time ≤ T
4. Take the minimum T over all candidate points

For two circles with centers (x1,y1), (x2,y2) and radii r1, r2:
- The intersection points can be calculated using standard geometric formulas
- The circles intersect if |r1 - r2| ≤ distance between centers ≤ r1 + r2
- If they intersect, there are 0, 1, or 2 solutions

Let me implement the circle intersection.

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circle_intersection(center1, center2, r1, r2):
    # centers, radii
    x1, y1 = center1
    x2, y2 = center2
    
    d = distance(center1, center2)
    
    # No intersection or one point
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return []
    
    if d == 0 and r1 == r2:
        # same center, same radius - infinite solutions
        # return any point, but we want a representative
        return [(x1, y1)]
    
    # Calculate intersection points
    # Line connecting centers
    a = (x2 - x1) / d
    b = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Midpoint of intersection
    mx = x1 + a * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    my = y1 + b * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    
    # Half the distance from line to intersection points
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - (d*d - r1*r1 + r2*r2)**2 / (4*d*d))
    
    # Two intersection points
    px1 = mx + b * h
    py1 = my - a * h
    px2 = mx - b * h
    py2 = my + a * h
    
    return [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

def solve():
    # Read input
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    n = int(data[0])
    k = int(data[1])
    
    meats = []
    idx = 2
    for i in range(n):
        x = int(data[idx]); y = int(data[idx+1]); c = int(data[idx+2])
        idx += 3
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # Generate candidate points
    candidates = []
    
    # Add all meat positions
    for x, y, c in meats:
        candidates.append((x, y))
    
    # Add intersection points of circles from pairs of pieces
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
            
            # Circle 1: center (x1,y1), radius T/c1
            # Circle 2: center (x2,y2), radius T/c2
            # But T is unknown - we need to find intersection at specific T, but T is what we're solving for
            
            # Actually, for a fixed T, we can compute intersection of circles with radius T/c1 and T/c2
            # But T varies, so we can't precompute.
            
            # This approach won't work directly.
            
            # Alternative: for each possible T during binary search, we can compute intersections
            # But that would make the candidate points depend on T.
            
            # So this approach is not valid.
    
    # Let's go back - use binary search on time T.
    
    # Binary search on time T
    # Lower bound: 0
    # Upper bound: find a safe upper bound
    # Maximum possible time: when we put the heat source at a point far away, but we want minimum time
    # When we put it at one of the meat positions, the time for the farthest piece is at most max(c_i) * max_distance_between_points
    max_dist = 0
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            d = distance(meats[i][:2], meats[j][:2])
            max_dist = max(max_dist, d)
    
    upper_bound = max(meats[i][2] for i in range(n)) * max_dist
    
    # But this is large, we can use binary search
    
    # For a given T, is there a point (X,Y) such that at least K pieces have c_i * distance <= T?
    # This is equivalent to: for each piece i, we have (X-x_i)^2 + (Y-y_i)^2 <= (T/c_i)^2
    
    # We need to check if there's a point (X,Y) satisfying this for at least K pieces.
    
    # One way: the feasible region for each piece is a disk.
    # We need to check if the union of at least K disks has a common point? No - we need a point that lies in at least K disks.
    
    # This is equivalent to: the intersection of the complements of at least K disks is empty? Not directly.
    
    # Actually, we need to check if there exists a point that is in at least K disks.
    
    # This is a complex geometric problem.
    
    # Given the constraints N ≤ 60, and that K is at least 1, we can try to use the fact that the optimal point is likely to be at a meat position or at an intersection of two circles.
    
    # But since T is unknown, we can try a different approach.
    
    # I'll use binary search on time, and for each T, use a geometric approach to check if there's a point that lies in at least K circles.
    
    # Since N is small (≤60), and K is at most 60, we can try to find if there's a point that lies in the intersection of any K circles, but that's exponential.
    
    # Another idea: the set of points that are within T/c_i of piece i is a disk. The condition is that there exists a point in the union of at least K such disks.
    
    # This is equivalent to: the covering number, but hard to compute.
    
    # After research, a known solution for such problems is to use ternary search or binary search with a spatial subdivision, but that's complex.
    
    # Given the time, I'll implement a solution that works for the sample and hope it generalizes.
    
    # Let me try to find the minimum time by checking only the meat positions as candidates.
    
    min_time = float('inf')
    
    # For each meat position as heat source
    for x, y, c in meats:
        times = []
        for mx, my, mc in meats:
            dist = math.sqrt((x - mx)**2 + (y - my)**2)
            times.append(mc * dist)
        
        # Time when K pieces are ready
        times_sorted = sorted(times)
        time_for_k_pieces = times_sorted[K-1]  # K-th smallest time
        min_time = min(min_time, time_for_k_pieces)
    
    # Also try the sample point from the problem
    # In the sample, (-0.2, 0) gives 2.4
    # Try to find better by checking intersection points with a specific T
    
    # Since this is complex, and the sample only has 4 pieces, let me try to implement a more complete solution with binary search.
    
    # Binary search on time
    left, right = 0.0, upper_bound
    # We need high precision
    for _ in range(100):  # 100 iterations gives about 1e-30 precision
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check if mid is feasible: is there a point (X,Y) such that at least K pieces have c_i * distance <= mid?
        # For each piece i, the condition is (X-x_i)^2 + (Y-y_i)^2 <= (mid/c_i)^2
        
        # We need to find if there's a point (X,Y) that satisfies this for at least K pieces.
        
        # One way is to check all pairwise intersection points of the circles with radius mid/c_i for each piece.
        # But there are C(n,2) = 15 for n=4, which is manageable.
        
        # For each pair of pieces, and for each intersection point of their two circles, check how many pieces are within mid/c_i distance.
        
        # But we need to consider that a point might be in the circle of piece i only if its distance to i is <= mid/c_i.
        
        # So for each intersection point of two circles (from pieces i and j), compute the distance to all other pieces and count how many are within mid/c_i.
        
        # Also add the meat positions themselves.
        
        candidates = []
        
        # Add all meat positions
        for x, y, c in meats:
            candidates.append((x, y))
        
        # Add intersection points of circles from pairs of pieces
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i+1, n):
                x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
                x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
                
                # Circle 1: center (x1,y1), radius = mid/c1
                # Circle 2: center (x2,y2), radius = mid/c2
                r1 = mid / c1
                r2 = mid / c2
                
                # Check if circles intersect
                d = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2)
                if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
                    continue
                    
                # Get intersection points
                intersection_points = circle_intersection((x1,y1), (x2,y2), r1, r2)
                for px, py in intersection_points:
                    candidates.append((px, py))
        
        # For each candidate point, count how many pieces are within distance mid/c_i
        feasible = False
        for px, py in candidates:
            count = 0
            for x, y, c in meats:
                dist = math.sqrt((px - x)**2 + (py - y)**2)
                if c * dist <= mid:
                    count += 1
            if count >= k:
                feasible = True
                break
                
        if feasible:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

solve()
```

But there's a bug in the circle_intersection function - it's not correctly implementing the formula.

Let me fix the circle intersection:

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circle_intersection(center1, center2, r1, r2):
    # centers, radii
    x1, y1 = center1
    x2, y2 = center2
    
    d = distance(center1, center2)
    
    # No intersection or one point
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return []
    
    if d == 0 and r1 == r2:
        # same center, same radius - infinite solutions
        return [(x1, y1)]
    
    # Calculate intersection points
    # Line connecting centers
    a = (x2 - x1) / d
    b = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Midpoint of intersection
    mx = x1 + a * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    my = y1 + b * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    
    # Half the distance from line to intersection points
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - (d*d - r1*r1 + r2*r2)**2 / (4*d*d))
    
    # Two intersection points
    px1 = mx + b * h
    py1 = my - a * h
    px2 = mx - b * h
    py2 = my + a * h
    
    return [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

def solve():
    # Read input
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    n = int(data[0])
    k = int(data[1])
    
    meats = []
    idx = 2
    for i in range(n):
        x = int(data[idx]); y = int(data[idx+1]); c = int(data[idx+2])
        idx += 3
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If k is 1, we can always achieve time 0 by placing heat source at any piece
    # But even for k=1, we need to find the minimum time when at least 1 piece is ready, which is 0.
    # Actually, if we place heat source at a piece, its cooking time is 0.
    # So for k=1, answer is 0.
    if k == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Generate candidate points
    candidates = []
    
    # Add all meat positions
    for x, y, c in meats:
        candidates.append((x, y))
    
    # Add intersection points of circles from pairs of pieces
    # For a point to be in circle i, distance to i <= mid/c_i
    # But mid is what we're solving for in binary search.
    # So in the binary search, for each mid, we will generate candidate points.
    
    # Binary search on time T
    # Lower bound: 0
    # Upper bound: find a safe upper bound
    max_dist = 0
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            d = distance(meats[i][:2], meats[j][:2])
            max_dist = max(max_dist, d)
    
    upper_bound = max(c for x,y,c in meats) * max_dist
    
    # Binary search for minimum time
    left, right = 0.0, upper_bound
    # 100 iterations should give precision of 1e-30
    for _ in range(100):
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check if mid is feasible: is there a point (X,Y) such that at least k pieces have c_i * distance <= mid?
        # Find candidate points: meat positions and intersection points of circles of radius mid/c_i
        feasible = False
        
        # Generate all candidate points for this mid
        candidate_points = []
        
        # Add all meat positions
        for x, y, c in meats:
            candidate_points.append((x, y))
        
        # Add intersection points of pairs of circles
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i+1, n):
                x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
                x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
                
                r1 = mid / c1
                r2 = mid / c2
                
                d = distance((x1,y1), (x2,y2))
                if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
                    continue
                    
                intersection_points = circle_intersection((x1,y1), (x2,y2), r1, r2)
                for px, py in intersection_points:
                    candidate_points.append((px, py))
        
        # For each candidate point, count how many pieces are within mid/c_i distance
        for px, py in candidate_points:
            count = 0
            for x, y, c in meats:
                dist = math.sqrt((px - x)**2 + (py - y)**2)
                if c * dist <= mid:
                    count += 1
            if count >= k:
                feasible = True
                break
        
        if feasible:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")

solve()
```

Let me fix the circle intersection formula - I think there's a bug in the h calculation.

The correct formula for h:

h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - (d*d - r1*r1 + r2*r2)**2 / (4*d*d))

Yes, that's correct.

Actually, let me double-check: the distance from the center line to the intersection points is h = sqrt(r1^2 - (distance from center to midpoint)^2)

distance from center to midpoint is (d^2 - r1^2 + r2^2)/(2d)

So h = sqrt(r1^2 - ((d^2 - r1^2 + r2^2)/(2d))^2)

Which is h = sqrt(r1^2 - (d^2 - r1^2 + r2^2)^2 / (4*d^2))

Yes, correct.

The code should work.

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circle_intersection(center1, center2, r1, r2):
    # centers, radii
    x1, y1 = center1
    x2, y2 = center2
    
    d = distance(center1, center2)
    
    # No intersection or one point
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return []
    
    if d == 0 and r1 == r2:
        # same center, same radius - infinite solutions
        return [(x1, y1)]
    
    # Calculate intersection points
    # Line connecting centers
    a = (x2 - x1) / d
    b = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Midpoint of intersection
    mx = x1 + a * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    my = y1 + b * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    
    # Half the distance from line to intersection points
    # h = sqrt(r1^2 - ((d^2 - r1^2 + r2^2)/(2d))^2)
    numerator = d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2
    if d == 0:
        return [(x1, y1)]
    d_sq = d * d
    h_sq = r1 * r1 - (numerator * numerator) / (4 * d_sq)
    if h_sq < 0:
        return []
    h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
    
    # Two intersection points
    px1 = mx + b * h
    py1 = my - a * h
    px2 = mx - b * h
    py2 = my + a * h
    
    return [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

def solve():
    # Read input
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read
    data = input().split()
    
    n = int(data[0])
    k = int(data[1])
    
    meats = []
    idx = 2
    for i in range(n):
        x = int(data[idx]); y = int(data[idx+1]); c = int(data[idx+2])
        idx += 3
        meats.append((x, y, c))
    
    # If k is 1, we can achieve time 0 by placing heat source at any piece
    if k == 1:
        print("0.0")
        return
    
    # Upper bound for time
    max_dist = 0
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            d = distance(meats[i][:2], meats[j][:2])
            max_dist = max(max_dist, d)
    
    upper_bound = max(c for x,y,c in meats) * max_dist
    
    # Binary search on time T
    left, right = 0.0, upper_bound
    # 100 iterations for high precision
    for _ in range(100):
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        
        # Check if mid is feasible: at least k pieces are ready by time mid
        feasible = False
        
        # Candidate points: meat positions and intersection points of circles
        candidate_points = []
        
        # Add meat positions
        for x, y, c in meats:
            candidate_points.append((x, y))
        
        # Add intersection points of pairs of circles with radius mid/c_i
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i+1, n):
                x1, y1, c1 = meats[i]
                x2, y2, c2 = meats[j]
                
                r1 = mid / c1
                r2 = mid / c2
                
                d = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
                if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
                    continue
                    
                intersection_points = circle_intersection((x1, y1), (x2, y2), r1, r2)
                for px, py in intersection_points:
                    candidate_points.append((px, py))
        
        # Check each candidate point
        for px, py in candidate_points:
            count = 0
            for x, y, c in meats:
                dist = math.sqrt((px - x)**2 + (py - y)**2)
                if c * dist <= mid:
                    count += 1
            if count >= k:
                feasible = True
                break
        
        if feasible:
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.15f}")
```

import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circle_intersection(center1, center2, r1, r2):
    # centers, radii
    x1, y1 = center1
    x2, y2 = center2
    
    d = distance(center1, center2)
    
    # No intersection or one point
    if d > r1 + r2 or d < abs(r1 - r2):
        return []
    
    if d == 0 and r1 == r2:
        # same center, same radius - infinite solutions
        return [(x1, y1)]
    
    # Calculate intersection points
    # Line connecting centers
    a = (x2 - x1) / d
    b = (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Midpoint of intersection
    mx = x1 + a * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    my = y1 + b * (d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2) / (2 * d)
    
    # Half the distance from line to intersection points
    # h = sqrt(r1^2 - ((d^2 - r1^2 + r2^2)/(2d))^2)
    numerator = d * d - r1 * r1 + r2 * r2
    if d == 0:
        return [(x1, y1)]
    d_sq = d * d
    h_sq = r1 * r1 - (numerator * numerator) / (4 * d_sq)
    if h_sq < 0:
        return []
    h = math.sqrt(h_sq)
    
    # Two intersection points
    px1 = mx + b * h
    py1 = my - a * h
    px2 = mx - b * h
    py2 = my + a * h
    
    return [(px1, py1), (px2, py2)]

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Takahashi wants to grill N pieces of meat on a grilling net, which can be seen as a two-dimensional plane. The coordinates of the i-th piece of meat are \left(x_i, y_i\right), and its hardness is c_i.
Takahashi can use one heat source to grill the meat. If he puts the heat source at coordinates \left(X, Y\right), where X and Y are real numbers, the i-th piece of meat will be ready to eat in c_i \times \sqrt{\left(X - x_i\right)^2 + \left(Y-y_i\right)^2} seconds.
Takahashi wants to eat K pieces of meat. Find the time required to have K or more pieces of meat ready if he put the heat source to minimize this time.

-----Constraints-----
 - All values in input are integers.
 - 1 \leq N \leq 60
 - 1 \leq K \leq N
 - -1000 \leq x_i , y_i \leq 1000
 - \left(x_i, y_i\right) \neq \left(x_j, y_j\right) \left(i \neq j \right)
 - 1 \leq c_i \leq 100

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N K
x_1 y_1 c_1
\vdots
x_N y_N c_N

-----Output-----
Print the answer.
It will be considered correct if its absolute or relative error from our answer is at most 10^{-6}.

-----Sample Input-----
4 3
-1 0 3
0 0 3
1 0 2
1 1 40

-----Sample Output-----
2.4

If we put the heat source at \left(-0.2, 0\right), the 1-st, 2-nd, and 3-rd pieces of meat will be ready to eat within 2.4 seconds. This is the optimal place to put the heat source.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

1. We have N pieces of meat at coordinates (x_i, y_i) with hardness c_i

3. We want to find the minimum time such that at least K pieces are ready
4. We need to minimize this time by optimally placing the heat source

For the intersection of K circles, a point exists if and only if the intersection of all pairs of circles is non-empty and the intersection of K-1 circles (for any choice) contains the K-th circle or something similar.

But to keep it simple and given the constraints, I'll implement a binary search with a feasibility function that checks if there's a point in the intersection of a subset of circles.

Actually, let me use a different approach: the optimal solution will have K pieces at exactly time T, so the heat source is positioned such that c_i * distance = T for those K pieces. This means the distance = T/c_i for those pieces.

So I need to find a point (X, Y) that is at distance T/c_i from piece i for at least K pieces.

For a fixed T, this is equivalent to finding if there's a point in the intersection of at least K circles.

I'll implement binary search on T and for each T, check if there's any point in the intersection of at least K circles.

For efficiency, I'll only consider combinations of up to 4 circles since their intersection can be computed, and if such an intersection exists, it might work for K pieces.

But that's not guaranteed.

Given the complexity and the sample size, I'll implement a binary search solution with geometric intersection checking for small K.

Actually, let me solve it with a simpler observation: we can use binary search on T, and for each T, we can create a function that checks if there's a point (X, Y) such that at least K pieces satisfy the constraint.

To check this, I can try all pairwise intersections of circles from all pieces, and for each such intersection point, count how many pieces satisfy the constraint.

If for any intersection point, at least K pieces satisfy the constraint, then T is feasible.

Additionally, I should also check the meat locations themselves.

So the plan:
1. Binary search on T from 0 to a large upper bound (e.g., 10000)
2. For each T, generate all pairwise intersection points of circles defined by (x_i, y_i) with radius T/c_i for all pieces
3. For each such intersection point (or meat locations), count how many pieces satisfy distance ≤ T/c_i
4. If any point has at least K pieces satisfying the constraint, T is feasible
5. Return the minimum such T

Let me implement this.

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circle_intersection(c1, c2):
    # c1 = (x1, y1, r1), c2 = (x2, y2, r2)
    x1, y1, r1 = c1
    x2, y2, r2 = c2
    
    d = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    if d >= r1 + r2 or d <= abs(r1 - r2):
        return []  # no intersection or one circle inside another
    
    # Calculate the intersection points
    a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - a*a)
    
    # Point on the line between centers
    x0 = x1 + a * (x2 - x1) / d
    y0 = y1 + a * (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Two intersection points
    x3 = x0 + h * (y2 - y1) / d
    y3 = y0 - h * (x2 - x1) / d
    x4 = x0 - h * (y2 - y1) / d
    y4 = y0 + h * (x2 - x1) / d
    
    return [(x3, y3), (x4, y4)]

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    return distance(point, center) <= radius

def feasible(T, pieces, K):
    # pieces is list of (x, y, c)
    n = len(pieces)
    points = set()
    
    # Add all meat locations
    for x, y, c in pieces:
        points.add((x, y))
    
    # Add all pairwise intersections of circles
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = pieces[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = pieces[j]
            r1 = T / c1
            r2 = T / c2
            
            intersections = circle_intersection((x1, y1, r1), (x2, y2, r2))
            for (x, y) in intersections:
                points.add((x, y))
    
    # For each candidate point, count how many pieces are within T/c_i
    for (x, y) in points:
        count = 0
        for px, py, pc in pieces:
            dist = distance((x, y), (px, py))
            if pc * dist <= T:
                count += 1
        if count >= K:
            return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    pieces = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        pieces.append((x, y, c))
    
    # Binary search on T
    left, right = 0.0, 10000.0
    
    # Binary search for minimum time
    eps = 1e-7
    while right - left > eps:
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if feasible(mid, pieces, K):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.10f}")

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be careful about the circle intersection function. When d=0 (same center), we have special case.

Let me fix the circle intersection function:

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def circle_intersection(c1, c2):
    # c1 = (x1, y1, r1), c2 = (x2, y2, r2)
    x1, y1, r1 = c1
    x2, y2, r2 = c2
    
    d = distance((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    if d >= r1 + r2:
        return []  # no intersection
    if d <= abs(r1 - r2):
        return []  # one circle inside another
    
    # Calculate the intersection points
    a = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
    h = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - a*a)
    
    # Point on the line between centers
    x0 = x1 + a * (x2 - x1) / d
    y0 = y1 + a * (y2 - y1) / d
    
    # Two intersection points
    x3 = x0 + h * (y2 - y1) / d
    y3 = y0 - h * (x2 - x1) / d
    x4 = x0 - h * (y2 - y1) / d
    y4 = y0 + h * (x2 - x1) / d
    
    return [(x3, y3), (x4, y4)]

def point_in_circle(point, center, radius):
    return distance(point, center) <= radius

def feasible(T, pieces, K):
    # pieces is list of (x, y, c)
    n = len(pieces)
    points = set()
    
    # Add all meat locations
    for x, y, c in pieces:
        points.add((x, y))
    
    # Add all pairwise intersections of circles
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            x1, y1, c1 = pieces[i]
            x2, y2, c2 = pieces[j]
            r1 = T / c1
            r2 = T / c2
            
            intersections = circle_intersection((x1, y1, r1), (x2, y2, r2))
            for (x, y) in intersections:
                points.add((x, y))
    
    # For each candidate point, count how many pieces are within T/c_i
    for (x, y) in points:
        count = 0
        for px, py, pc in pieces:
            dist = distance((x, y), (px, py))
            if pc * dist <= T:
                count += 1
        if count >= K:
            return True
    
    return False

def solve():
    N, K = map(int, input().split())
    pieces = []
    for _ in range(N):
        x, y, c = map(int, input().split())
        pieces.append((x, y, c))
    
    # Binary search on T
    left, right = 0.0, 10000.0
    
    # Binary search for minimum time
    eps = 1e-7
    while right - left > eps:
        mid = (left + right) / 2.0
        if feasible(mid, pieces, K):
            right = mid
        else:
            left = mid
    
    print(f"{left:.10f}")

solve()
```

For piece 0: distance ≤ 2.4/3 = 0.8
For piece 1: distance ≤ 2.4/3 = 0.8  
For piece 2: distance ≤ 2.4/2 = 1.2
For piece 3: distance ≤ 2.4/40 = 0.06
